Substituting this into the second equation:

$7|\bar{a}|^2 - 30\left(\dfrac{1}{2}|\bar{b}|^2\right) + 8|\bar{b}|^2 = 0$

$7|\bar{a}|^2 - 15|\bar{b}|^2 + 8|\bar{b}|^2 = 0$

$7|\bar{a}|^2 - 7|\bar{b}|^2 = 0 \Rightarrow |\bar{a}| = |\bar{b}|$

Let $\theta$ be the angle between $\bar{a}$ and $\bar{b}$. Then,

$\cos \theta = \dfrac{\bar{a} \cdot \bar{b}}{|\bar{a}||\bar{b}|} = \dfrac{\dfrac{1}{2}|\bar{b}|^2}{|\bar{b}|^2} = \dfrac{1}{2}$

$\theta = \dfrac{\pi}{3}$

Answer: $\dfrac{\pi}{3}$

</details>

---

### Q51. MHT CET 2026 (11 April Shift 1)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `2`

If $7\hat{j} + 10\hat{k}, -\hat{i} + 6\hat{j} + 6\hat{k}$ and $-4\hat{i} + 9\hat{j} + 6\hat{k}$ are the position vectors of the vertices A, B and C repectively of $\triangle ABC$. Then the position vector of the point where the bisector of the angle A meets side BC is

**Options:**

- **(A)** $(2 + 3\sqrt{2})\hat{i} + (3 + 3\sqrt{3})\hat{j} + 6\hat{k}$
- **(B)** $(2 - 3\sqrt{2})\hat{i} + (3 + 3\sqrt{2})\hat{j} + 6\hat{k}$
- **(C)** $(2 - 3\sqrt{2})\hat{i} + (3 - 3\sqrt{2})\hat{j} + 6\hat{k}$
- **(D)** $(2 - 3\sqrt{2})\hat{i} + (3 + 3\sqrt{2})\hat{j} - 6\hat{k}$

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(B) $(2 - 3\sqrt{2})\hat{i} + (3 + 3\sqrt{2})\hat{j} + 6\hat{k}$**

**Detailed Solution:**

Let the position vectors of the vertices be $\vec{a} = 7\hat{j} + 10\hat{k}$, $\vec{b} = -\hat{i} + 6\hat{j} + 6\hat{k}$, and $\vec{c} = -4\hat{i} + 9\hat{j} + 6\hat{k}$.

First, we find the vectors $\overrightarrow{AB}$ and $\overrightarrow{AC}$ and their magnitudes:
$\overrightarrow{AB} = \vec{b} - \vec{a} = (-\hat{i} + 6\hat{j} + 6\hat{k}) - (7\hat{j} + 10\hat{k}) = -\hat{i} - \hat{j} - 4\hat{k}$
$|\overrightarrow{AB}| = \sqrt{(-1)^2 + (-1)^2 + (-4)^2} = \sqrt{1 + 1 + 16} = \sqrt{18} = 3\sqrt{2}$

$\overrightarrow{AC} = \vec{c} - \vec{a} = (-4\hat{i} + 9\hat{j} + 6\hat{k}) - (7\hat{j} + 10\hat{k}) = -4\hat{i} + 2\hat{j} - 4\hat{k}$
$|\overrightarrow{AC}| = \sqrt{(-4)^2 + 2^2 + (-4)^2} = \sqrt{16 + 4 + 16} = \sqrt{36} = 6$

Let $D$ be the point where the bisector of angle $A$ meets the side $BC$. By the angle bisector theorem, $D$ divides $BC$ internally in the ratio of the adjacent sides:
$\dfrac{BD}{DC} = \dfrac{|\overrightarrow{AB}|}{|\overrightarrow{AC}|} = \dfrac{3\sqrt{2}}{6} = \dfrac{1}{\sqrt{2}}$

Using the section formula, the position vector of $D$ is:
$\vec{d} = \dfrac{\sqrt{2}\vec{b} + 1\vec{c}}{1 + \sqrt{2}}$

Substituting $\vec{b}$ and $\vec{c}$:
$\vec{d} = \dfrac{\sqrt{2}(-\hat{i} + 6\hat{j} + 6\hat{k}) + (-4\hat{i} + 9\hat{j} + 6\hat{k})}{\sqrt{2} + 1}$
$\vec{d} = \dfrac{(-\sqrt{2} - 4)\hat{i} + (6\sqrt{2} + 9)\hat{j} + (6\sqrt{2} + 6)\hat{k}}{\sqrt{2} + 1}$

To rationalize the denominator, multiply the numerator and denominator by $(\sqrt{2} - 1)$:
$\vec{d} = \dfrac{[(-\sqrt{2} - 4)\hat{i} + (6\sqrt{2} + 9)\hat{j} + (6\sqrt{2} + 6)\hat{k}](\sqrt{2} - 1)}{(\sqrt{2} + 1)(\sqrt{2} - 1)}$

Since $(\sqrt{2} + 1)(\sqrt{2} - 1) = 2 - 1 = 1$, we simplify the numerator:
For $\hat{i}$: $(-\sqrt{2} - 4)(\sqrt{2} - 1) = -2 + \sqrt{2} - 4\sqrt{2} + 4 = 2 - 3\sqrt{2}$
For $\hat{j}$: $(6\sqrt{2} + 9)(\sqrt{2} - 1) = 12 - 6\sqrt{2} + 9\sqrt{2} - 9 = 3 + 3\sqrt{2}$
For $\hat{k}$: $(6\sqrt{2} + 6)(\sqrt{2} - 1) = 12 - 6\sqrt{2} + 6\sqrt{2} - 6 = 6$

Therefore, the position vector of $D$ is:
$\vec{d} = (2 - 3\sqrt{2})\hat{i} + (3 + 3\sqrt{2})\hat{j} + 6\hat{k}$

Answer: $(2 - 3\sqrt{2})\hat{i} + (3 + 3\sqrt{2})\hat{j} + 6\hat{k}$

</details>

---

### Q52. MHT CET 2026 (11 April Shift 1)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `2`

Let $A(2,3,0)$, $B(0,3,2)$ and $C(4,0,3)$ be vertices of a triangle, then the area of the triangle is

**Options:**

- **(A)** $\dfrac{\sqrt{171}}{2} \text{ sq. units}$
- **(B)** $\dfrac{\sqrt{172}}{2} \text{ sq. units}$
- **(C)** $\dfrac{\sqrt{173}}{2} \text{ sq. units}$
- **(D)** $\dfrac{\sqrt{174}}{2} \text{ sq. units}$

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(B) $\dfrac{\sqrt{172}}{2} \text{ sq. units}$**

**Detailed Solution:**

The position vectors of the vertices are $A(2,3,0)$, $B(0,3,2)$, and $C(4,0,3)$.

The vectors representing the sides $\overrightarrow{AB}$ and $\overrightarrow{AC}$ are:
$\overrightarrow{AB} = (0 - 2)\hat{i} + (3 - 3)\hat{j} + (2 - 0)\hat{k} = -2\hat{i} + 0\hat{j} + 2\hat{k}$
$\overrightarrow{AC} = (4 - 2)\hat{i} + (0 - 3)\hat{j} + (3 - 0)\hat{k} = 2\hat{i} - 3\hat{j} + 3\hat{k}$

The area of the triangle is given by $\dfrac{1}{2} |\overrightarrow{AB} \times \overrightarrow{AC}|$.

Calculating the cross product:
$\overrightarrow{AB} \times \overrightarrow{AC} = \begin{vmatrix} \hat{i} & \hat{j} & \hat{k} \\ -2 & 0 & 2 \\ 2 & -3 & 3 \end{vmatrix}$

$\overrightarrow{AB} \times \overrightarrow{AC} = \hat{i}(0 - (-6)) - \hat{j}(-6 - 4) + \hat{k}(6 - 0)$
$\overrightarrow{AB} \times \overrightarrow{AC} = 6\hat{i} + 10\hat{j} + 6\hat{k}$

Now, finding the magnitude of the cross product:
$|\overrightarrow{AB} \times \overrightarrow{AC}| = \sqrt{6^2 + 10^2 + 6^2} = \sqrt{36 + 100 + 36} = \sqrt{172}$

Therefore, the area of the triangle is:
$\text{Area} = \dfrac{1}{2} |\overrightarrow{AB} \times \overrightarrow{AC}| = \dfrac{\sqrt{172}}{2} \text{ sq. units}$

Answer: $\dfrac{\sqrt{172}}{2} \text{ sq. units}$

</details>

---

### Q53. MHT CET 2026 (11 April Shift 1)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `1`

If D and E are the midpoints of the sides BA and BC of triangle ABC, then $\overline{AE} + \overline{DC} = $

**Options:**

- **(A)** $\overline{AC}$
- **(B)** $\dfrac{3}{2} \overline{BC}$
- **(C)** $\dfrac{3}{2} \overline{AC}$
- **(D)** $\dfrac{1}{2} \overline{AC}$

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(C) $\dfrac{3}{2} \overline{AC}$**

**Detailed Solution:**

Using the triangle law of vector addition:

$\overline{AE} = \overline{AB} + \overline{BE}$

$\overline{DC} = \overline{DB} + \overline{BC}$

Since D is the midpoint of BA, $\overline{DB} = \dfrac{1}{2}\overline{AB}$.

Since E is the midpoint of BC, $\overline{BE} = \dfrac{1}{2}\overline{BC}$.

Substituting these into the expressions for $\overline{AE}$ and $\overline{DC}$:

$\overline{AE} + \overline{DC} = \left(\overline{AB} + \dfrac{1}{2}\overline{BC}\right) + \left(\dfrac{1}{2}\overline{AB} + \overline{BC}\right)$

$\overline{AE} + \overline{DC} = \dfrac{3}{2}\overline{AB} + \dfrac{3}{2}\overline{BC}$

$\overline{AE} + \overline{DC} = \dfrac{3}{2}(\overline{AB} + \overline{BC})$

By the triangle law of vector addition, $\overline{AB} + \overline{BC} = \overline{AC}$.

$\overline{AE} + \overline{DC} = \dfrac{3}{2}\overline{AC}$

Answer: $\dfrac{3}{2} \overline{AC}$

</details>

---

### Q54. MHT CET 2025 (5 May Shift 2)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `2`

If $\bar{c}=5 \bar{a}+6 \bar{b}$ and $3 \bar{c}=\bar{a}-4 \bar{b}$ then

**Options:**

- **(A)** $\bar{a}, \bar{b}, \bar{c}$ are non-collinear
- **(B)** $\overline{\mathrm{a}}, \overline{\mathrm{b}}, \overline{\mathrm{c}}$ are in the same direction
- **(C)** $\overline{\mathrm{a}}, \overline{\mathrm{c}}$ are in the same direction but $\overline{\mathrm{a}}, \overline{\mathrm{b}}$ are in the opposite direction
- **(D)** $\bar{c}, \bar{b}$ are in the opposite direction and $\bar{a}, \bar{b}$ are in the same direction

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(C) $\overline{\mathrm{a}}, \overline{\mathrm{c}}$ are in the same direction but $\overline{\mathrm{a}}, \overline{\mathrm{b}}$ are in the opposite direction**

**Detailed Solution:**

Given equations are:
$\bar{c} = 5\bar{a} + 6\bar{b}$
$3\bar{c} = \bar{a} - 4\bar{b}$

Substituting the first equation into the second equation:
$3(5\bar{a} + 6\bar{b}) = \bar{a} - 4\bar{b}$

$15\bar{a} + 18\bar{b} = \bar{a} - 4\bar{b}$

$14\bar{a} = -22\bar{b}$

$\bar{a} = -\dfrac{11}{7}\bar{b}$

Since the scalar multiple is negative, $\bar{a}$ and $\bar{b}$ are collinear and in opposite directions.

Now, substituting $\bar{a}$ into the expression for $\bar{c}$:
$\bar{c} = 5\left(-\dfrac{11}{7}\bar{b}\right) + 6\bar{b}$

$\bar{c} = -\dfrac{55}{7}\bar{b} + \dfrac{42}{7}\bar{b}$

$\bar{c} = -\dfrac{13}{7}\bar{b}$

Since the scalar multiple is negative, $\bar{c}$ and $\bar{b}$ are also in opposite directions.

Comparing $\bar{a}$ and $\bar{c}$:
$\bar{a} = -\dfrac{11}{7}\bar{b}$ and $\bar{c} = -\dfrac{13}{7}\bar{b}$

$\bar{a} = \dfrac{11}{13}\bar{c}$

Since the scalar multiple $\dfrac{11}{13}$ is positive, $\bar{a}$ and $\bar{c}$ are in the same direction.

Therefore, $\bar{a}$ and $\bar{c}$ are in the same direction but $\bar{a}$ and $\bar{b}$ are in opposite directions.

Answer: $\overline{\mathrm{a}}, \overline{\mathrm{c}}$ are in the same direction but $\overline{\mathrm{a}}, \overline{\mathrm{b}}$ are in the opposite direction

</details>

---

### Q55. MHT CET 2025 (5 May Shift 2)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `2`

ABCD is a quadrilateral with $\overline{\mathrm{AB}}=\bar{a}, \overline{\mathrm{AD}}=\overline{\mathrm{b}}$ and $\overline{\mathrm{AC}}=2 \bar{a}+3 \overline{\mathrm{~b}}$. If its area is $\alpha$ times the area of the parallelogram with $\mathrm{AB}, \mathrm{AD}$ as adjacent sides, then the value of $\alpha$ is

**Options:**

- **(A)** $\frac{1}{2}$
- **(B)** $\frac{5}{2}$
- **(C)** $\frac{3}{2}$
- **(D)** 2

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(B) $\frac{5}{2}$**

**Detailed Solution:**

The area of the parallelogram with adjacent sides $\overrightarrow{AB}$ and $\overrightarrow{AD}$ is given by:
Area of parallelogram $= |\overrightarrow{AB} \times \overrightarrow{AD}| = |\vec{a} \times \vec{b}|$

The area of the quadrilateral ABCD can be found using its diagonals $\overrightarrow{AC}$ and $\overrightarrow{BD}$.
First, we find the vector for the diagonal $\overrightarrow{BD}$:
$\overrightarrow{BD} = \overrightarrow{AD} - \overrightarrow{AB} = \vec{b} - \vec{a}$

The area of the quadrilateral is:
Area of ABCD $= \dfrac{1}{2} |\overrightarrow{AC} \times \overrightarrow{BD}|$

Substitute the given vectors $\overrightarrow{AC} = 2\vec{a} + 3\vec{b}$ and $\overrightarrow{BD} = \vec{b} - \vec{a}$:
Area of ABCD $= \dfrac{1}{2} |(2\vec{a} + 3\vec{b}) \times (\vec{b} - \vec{a})|$

Expanding the cross product:
Area of ABCD $= \dfrac{1}{2} |2(\vec{a} \times \vec{b}) - 2(\vec{a} \times \vec{a}) + 3(\vec{b} \times \vec{b}) - 3(\vec{b} \times \vec{a})|$

Since the cross product of a vector with itself is zero ($\vec{a} \times \vec{a} = \vec{0}$ and $\vec{b} \times \vec{b} = \vec{0}$) and $\vec{b} \times \vec{a} = -(\vec{a} \times \vec{b})$:
Area of ABCD $= \dfrac{1}{2} |2(\vec{a} \times \vec{b}) - \vec{0} + \vec{0} + 3(\vec{a} \times \vec{b})|$

Area of ABCD $= \dfrac{1}{2} |5(\vec{a} \times \vec{b})| = \dfrac{5}{2} |\vec{a} \times \vec{b}|$

Comparing this with the area of the parallelogram, we get:
Area of ABCD $= \dfrac{5}{2} \times$ (Area of parallelogram)

Therefore, $\alpha = \dfrac{5}{2}$.

Answer: $\frac{5}{2}$

</details>

---

### Q56. MHT CET 2025 (27 Apr Shift 2)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `2`

Let ABCD be a quadrilateral with $\overline{\mathrm{AB}}=\bar{a}, \overline{\mathrm{AD}}=\overline{\mathrm{b}}$ and $\overline{\mathrm{AC}}=3 \bar{a}+2 \overline{\mathrm{~b}}$. If its area is $\alpha$ times the area of the parallelogram with $\mathrm{AB}, \mathrm{AD}$ as adjacent sides, then the value of $\alpha$ is equal to

**Options:**

- **(A)** $\frac{5}{2}$
- **(B)** 4
- **(C)** 2
- **(D)** 1

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(A) $\frac{5}{2}$**

**Detailed Solution:**

The area of a quadrilateral ABCD is given by $\dfrac{1}{2} |\overrightarrow{AC} \times \overrightarrow{BD}|$.

The diagonal $\overrightarrow{BD}$ is given by $\overrightarrow{AD} - \overrightarrow{AB} = \vec{b} - \vec{a}$.

Taking the cross product of the diagonals:

$\overrightarrow{AC} \times \overrightarrow{BD} = (3\vec{a} + 2\vec{b}) \times (\vec{b} - \vec{a})$

$\overrightarrow{AC} \times \overrightarrow{BD} = 3(\vec{a} \times \vec{b}) - 3(\vec{a} \times \vec{a}) + 2(\vec{b} \times \vec{b}) - 2(\vec{b} \times \vec{a})$

Since $\vec{a} \times \vec{a} = \vec{0}$, $\vec{b} \times \vec{b} = \vec{0}$, and $\vec{b} \times \vec{a} = -(\vec{a} \times \vec{b})$:

$\overrightarrow{AC} \times \overrightarrow{BD} = 3(\vec{a} \times \vec{b}) + 2(\vec{a} \times \vec{b}) = 5(\vec{a} \times \vec{b})$

The area of the quadrilateral ABCD is $\dfrac{1}{2} |5(\vec{a} \times \vec{b})| = \dfrac{5}{2} |\vec{a} \times \vec{b}|$.

The area of the parallelogram with adjacent sides AB and AD is $|\overrightarrow{AB} \times \overrightarrow{AD}| = |\vec{a} \times \vec{b}|$.

Therefore, the area of the quadrilateral is $\dfrac{5}{2}$ times the area of the parallelogram, giving $\alpha = \dfrac{5}{2}$.

Answer: $\frac{5}{2}$

</details>

---

### Q57. MHT CET 2025 (27 Apr Shift 2)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `1`

If $4 \hat{i}+7 \hat{j}+8 \hat{k}, 2 \hat{i}+3 \hat{j}+4 \hat{k}$ and $2 \hat{i}+5 \hat{j}+7 \hat{k}$ are the position vectors of the vertices $\mathrm{A}, \mathrm{B}$ and C , respectively, of triangle ABC , then the position vector of the point in which bisector of $\angle B$ meets $C A$ is

**Options:**

- **(A)** $\frac{\hat{i}(4 \sqrt{13}+12)+\hat{j}(7 \sqrt{13}+30)+\hat{k}(8 \sqrt{13}+42)}{\sqrt{13}+6}$
- **(B)** $\hat{i}(4 \sqrt{13}+12)+\hat{j}(7 \sqrt{13}+30)+\hat{k}(8 \sqrt{3}+42)$
- **(C)** $\frac{\hat{i}(4 \sqrt{13}+12)+\hat{j}(7 \sqrt{13}+30)+\hat{k}(8 \sqrt{13}+42)}{\sqrt{13}-6}$
- **(D)** $\frac{\hat{i}(4 \sqrt{13}+12)+\hat{j}(7 \sqrt{13}+30)-\hat{k}(8 \sqrt{13}+42)}{6-\sqrt{13}}$

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(A) $\frac{\hat{i}(4 \sqrt{13}+12)+\hat{j}(7 \sqrt{13}+30)+\hat{k}(8 \sqrt{13}+42)}{\sqrt{13}+6}$**

**Detailed Solution:**

Let the position vectors of the vertices $A$, $B$, and $C$ be $\vec{a}$, $\vec{b}$, and $\vec{c}$ respectively.

$\vec{a} = 4\hat{i} + 7\hat{j} + 8\hat{k}$
$\vec{b} = 2\hat{i} + 3\hat{j} + 4\hat{k}$
$\vec{c} = 2\hat{i} + 5\hat{j} + 7\hat{k}$

The lengths of the sides $AB$ and $BC$ are given by:
$AB = |\vec{a} - \vec{b}| = |(4-2)\hat{i} + (7-3)\hat{j} + (8-4)\hat{k}| = |2\hat{i} + 4\hat{j} + 4\hat{k}|$
$AB = \sqrt{2^2 + 4^2 + 4^2} = \sqrt{4 + 16 + 16} = \sqrt{36} = 6$

$BC = |\vec{c} - \vec{b}| = |(2-2)\hat{i} + (5-3)\hat{j} + (7-4)\hat{k}| = |0\hat{i} + 2\hat{j} + 3\hat{k}|$
$BC = \sqrt{0^2 + 2^2 + 3^2} = \sqrt{4 + 9} = \sqrt{13}$

Let the internal bisector of $\angle B$ meet $AC$ at point $D$. By the angle bisector theorem, $D$ divides the segment $AC$ internally in the ratio of the adjacent sides:
$\dfrac{AD}{DC} = \dfrac{AB}{BC} = \dfrac{6}{\sqrt{13}}$

Using the section formula, the position vector of $D$ is:
$\vec{d} = \dfrac{\sqrt{13}\vec{a} + 6\vec{c}}{6 + \sqrt{13}}$

Substituting the position vectors $\vec{a}$ and $\vec{c}$:
$\vec{d} = \dfrac{\sqrt{13}(4\hat{i} + 7\hat{j} + 8\hat{k}) + 6(2\hat{i} + 5\hat{j} + 7\hat{k})}{\sqrt{13} + 6}$

$\vec{d} = \dfrac{(4\sqrt{13} + 12)\hat{i} + (7\sqrt{13} + 30)\hat{j} + (8\sqrt{13} + 42)\hat{k}}{\sqrt{13} + 6}$

Answer: $\frac{\hat{i}(4 \sqrt{13}+12)+\hat{j}(7 \sqrt{13}+30)+\hat{k}(8 \sqrt{13}+42)}{\sqrt{13}+6}$

</details>

---

### Q58. MHT CET 2025 (26 Apr Shift 2)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `1`

<img width=300px src="https://cdn.quizrr.in/question-assets/mhtcet/mhtcet2025/OXyutJk1tUSFbbK7qzPpLD8XH4n0NcATi3_LePNxm8Q.original.fullsize.png">


In the above figure, P divides AC in the ratio 3:4 and Q divides BC in the ratio $4: 3$. Then $M$ divides $A Q$ in the ratio

**Options:**

- **(A)** 15:14
- **(B)** 29:13
- **(C)** 21:16
- **(D)** 28:9

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(C) 21:16**

**Detailed Solution:**

Let the position vectors of the vertices $A, B, C$ be $\vec{a}, \vec{b}, \vec{c}$ respectively.

Since $P$ divides $AC$ in the ratio $3:4$, the position vector of $P$ is:
$\vec{p} = \dfrac{4\vec{a} + 3\vec{c}}{3 + 4} = \dfrac{4\vec{a} + 3\vec{c}}{7}$

Since $Q$ divides $BC$ in the ratio $4:3$, the position vector of $Q$ is:
$\vec{q} = \dfrac{3\vec{b} + 4\vec{c}}{4 + 3} = \dfrac{3\vec{b} + 4\vec{c}}{7}$

Let $M$ divide $AQ$ in the ratio $\lambda:1$ and $BP$ in the ratio $\mu:1$.
The position vector of $M$ can be written in two ways:

Using segment $AQ$:
$\vec{m} = \dfrac{\lambda\vec{q} + 1\vec{a}}{\lambda + 1} = \dfrac{\lambda\left(\dfrac{3\vec{b} + 4\vec{c}}{7}\right) + \vec{a}}{\lambda + 1} = \dfrac{7\vec{a} + 3\lambda\vec{b} + 4\lambda\vec{c}}{7(\lambda + 1)}$

Using segment $BP$:
$\vec{m} = \dfrac{\mu\vec{p} + 1\vec{b}}{\mu + 1} = \dfrac{\mu\left(\dfrac{4\vec{a} + 3\vec{c}}{7}\right) + \vec{b}}{\mu + 1} = \dfrac{4\mu\vec{a} + 7\vec{b} + 3\mu\vec{c}}{7(\mu + 1)}$

Since $\vec{a}, \vec{b}, \vec{c}$ are position vectors of non-collinear points, their coefficients in the two expressions for $\vec{m}$ must be equal. Equating the coefficients of $\vec{a}, \vec{b}, \vec{c}$:

$\dfrac{7}{7(\lambda + 1)} = \dfrac{4\mu}{7(\mu + 1)}$  ... (1)

$\dfrac{3\lambda}{7(\lambda + 1)} = \dfrac{7}{7(\mu + 1)}$ ... (2)

$\dfrac{4\lambda}{7(\lambda + 1)} = \dfrac{3\mu}{7(\mu + 1)}$ ... (3)

Dividing equation (3) by equation (1), we get:
$\dfrac{4\lambda}{7} = \dfrac{3\mu}{4\mu}$
$\dfrac{4\lambda}{7} = \dfrac{3}{4}$
$\lambda = \dfrac{21}{16}$

Therefore, $M$ divides $AQ$ in the ratio $21:16$.

Answer: 21:16

</details>

---

### Q59. MHT CET 2025 (26 Apr Shift 1)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `2`

If $\bar{a}=\lambda x \hat{i}+\mathrm{y} \hat{j}+4 \mathrm{z} \hat{k}, \overline{\mathrm{~b}}=\mathrm{y} \hat{i}+x \hat{\mathrm{j}}+3 \mathrm{y} \hat{\mathrm{k}}, \overline{\mathrm{c}}=-\mathrm{z} \hat{i}-2 \mathrm{z} \hat{\mathrm{j}}-(\lambda+1) \hat{\mathrm{k}} x$ are the sides of the triangle ABC , where $x, \mathrm{y}, \mathrm{z}$ are not all zero, such that $\bar{a}+\bar{b}-\bar{c}=\overline{0}$, then value of $\lambda$ is

**Options:**

- **(A)** 0
- **(B)** 1
- **(C)** 2
- **(D)** 3

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(A) 0**

**Detailed Solution:**

Given the vectors:

$\bar{a} = \lambda x \hat{i} + y \hat{j} + 4z \hat{k}$

$\bar{b} = y \hat{i} + x \hat{j} + 3y \hat{k}$

$\bar{c} = -z \hat{i} - 2z \hat{j} - (\lambda+1)x \hat{k}$

We are given the condition $\bar{a} + \bar{b} - \bar{c} = \overline{0}$. Substituting the vectors into this equation and grouping the $\hat{i}$, $\hat{j}$, and $\hat{k}$ components, we get:

$(\lambda x + y + z)\hat{i} + (x + y + 2z)\hat{j} + ((\lambda+1)x + 3y + 4z)\hat{k} = \overline{0}$

Equating the components to zero, we obtain a system of homogeneous linear equations in $x$, $y$, and $z$:

$\lambda x + y + z = 0$

$x + y + 2z = 0$

$(\lambda+1)x + 3y + 4z = 0$

Since $x$, $y$, and $z$ are not all zero, the system must have a non-trivial solution. Therefore, the determinant of the coefficient matrix must be zero:

$\begin{vmatrix} \lambda & 1 & 1 \\ 1 & 1 & 2 \\ \lambda+1 & 3 & 4 \end{vmatrix} = 0$

Expanding the determinant along the first row:

$\lambda(1 \times 4 - 2 \times 3) - 1(1 \times 4 - 2(\lambda+1)) + 1(1 \times 3 - 1(\lambda+1)) = 0$

$\lambda(4 - 6) - (4 - 2\lambda - 2) + (3 - \lambda - 1) = 0$

$-2\lambda - (2 - 2\lambda) + (2 - \lambda) = 0$

$-2\lambda - 2 + 2\lambda + 2 - \lambda = 0$

$-\lambda = 0$

$\lambda = 0$

Answer: 0

</details>

---

### Q60. MHT CET 2025 (23 Apr Shift 1)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `2`

Let $\bar{a}=2 \hat{i}+\hat{j}+\hat{k}, \bar{b}=\hat{i}+2 \hat{j}-\hat{k}$ and vector $\bar{c}$ be coplanar. If $\bar{c}$ is perpendicular to $\bar{a}$, then $\overline{\mathrm{c}}$ is

**Options:**

- **(A)** $-\hat{i}+2 \hat{\mathrm{k}}$
- **(B)** $-\hat{i}+\hat{j}+\hat{k}$
- **(C)** $\hat{i}-2 \hat{j}$
- **(D)** $-\hat{\mathrm{j}}+\hat{\mathrm{k}}$

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(D) $-\hat{\mathrm{j}}+\hat{\mathrm{k}}$**

**Detailed Solution:**

Since $\bar{c}$ is coplanar with $\bar{a}$ and $\bar{b}$, we can express it as a linear combination of $\bar{a}$ and $\bar{b}$:

$\bar{c} = x\bar{a} + y\bar{b}$

Given that $\bar{c}$ is perpendicular to $\bar{a}$, their dot product must be zero:

$\bar{c} \cdot \bar{a} = 0$

$(x\bar{a} + y\bar{b}) \cdot \bar{a} = 0$

$x(\bar{a} \cdot \bar{a}) + y(\bar{b} \cdot \bar{a}) = 0$

We calculate the required dot products:

$\bar{a} \cdot \bar{a} = (2)^2 + (1)^2 + (1)^2 = 4 + 1 + 1 = 6$

$\bar{b} \cdot \bar{a} = (1)(2) + (2)(1) + (-1)(1) = 2 + 2 - 1 = 3$

Substituting these values into the equation:

$6x + 3y = 0 \Rightarrow y = -2x$

Therefore, the vector $\bar{c}$ becomes:

$\bar{c} = x\bar{a} - 2x\bar{b} = x(\bar{a} - 2\bar{b})$

Now, we evaluate $\bar{a} - 2\bar{b}$:

$\bar{a} - 2\bar{b} = (2\hat{i} + \hat{j} + \hat{k}) - 2(\hat{i} + 2\hat{j} - \hat{k})$

$\bar{a} - 2\bar{b} = (2 - 2)\hat{i} + (1 - 4)\hat{j} + (1 + 2)\hat{k} = -3\hat{j} + 3\hat{k}$

Thus, $\bar{c} = x(-3\hat{j} + 3\hat{k}) = 3x(-\hat{j} + \hat{k})$.

For $3x = 1$, we get $\bar{c} = -\hat{j} + \hat{k}$, which is a valid vector satisfying all the given conditions.

Answer: $-\hat{\mathrm{j}}+\hat{\mathrm{k}}$

</details>

---

### Q61. MHT CET 2025 (22 Apr Shift 2)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `2`

The number of integral values of $p$ for which the vector $(\mathrm{p}+1) \hat{i}-3 \hat{\mathrm{j}}+\mathrm{p} \widehat{\mathrm{k}}, \mathrm{p} \hat{i}+(\mathrm{p}+1) \hat{\mathrm{j}}-3 \widehat{\mathrm{k}}$ and $-3 \hat{i}+\mathrm{p} \hat{\mathrm{j}}+(\mathrm{p}+1) \widehat{\mathrm{k}}$ are linearly dependent vectors, are

**Options:**

- **(A)** $0$
- **(B)** $1$
- **(C)** $2$
- **(D)** $3$

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(B) $1$**

**Detailed Solution:**

For three vectors to be linearly dependent, their scalar triple product (or the determinant of their components) must be zero.

$\begin{vmatrix} p+1 & -3 & p \\ p & p+1 & -3 \\ -3 & p & p+1 \end{vmatrix} = 0$

Applying the column operation $C_1 \rightarrow C_1 + C_2 + C_3$, we get:

$\begin{vmatrix} 2p-2 & -3 & p \\ 2p-2 & p+1 & -3 \\ 2p-2 & p & p+1 \end{vmatrix} = 0$

Taking $(2p-2)$ common from the first column:

$(2p-2) \begin{vmatrix} 1 & -3 & p \\ 1 & p+1 & -3 \\ 1 & p & p+1 \end{vmatrix} = 0$

Applying row operations $R_2 \rightarrow R_2 - R_1$ and $R_3 \rightarrow R_3 - R_1$:

$(2p-2) \begin{vmatrix} 1 & -3 & p \\ 0 & p+4 & -p-3 \\ 0 & p+3 & 1 \end{vmatrix} = 0$

Expanding along the first column:

$(2p-2) [ (p+4)(1) - (-p-3)(p+3) ] = 0$

$(2p-2) [ p+4 + (p+3)^2 ] = 0$

$(2p-2) [ p+4 + p^2 + 6p + 9 ] = 0$

$(2p-2) ( p^2 + 7p + 13 ) = 0$

For the quadratic equation $p^2 + 7p + 13 = 0$, the discriminant is $\Delta = 7^2 - 4(1)(13) = 49 - 52 = -3 < 0$. Thus, it has no real roots.

The only real root comes from $2p - 2 = 0 \Rightarrow p = 1$.

Since $p = 1$ is an integer, there is exactly $1$ integral value of $p$ for which the vectors are linearly dependent.

Answer: $1$

</details>

---

### Q62. MHT CET 2025 (22 Apr Shift 1)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `2`

If the vectors $m \hat{i}+m \hat{j}+n \hat{k}, \hat{i}+\hat{k}, n \hat{i}+n \hat{j}+p \hat{k}$ lie in a plane then...

**Options:**

- **(A)** $m+n+p=0$
- **(B)** $\mathrm{m}, \mathrm{n}, \mathrm{p}$ are in A.P.
- **(C)** $\mathrm{m}, \mathrm{n}, \mathrm{p}$ are in G.P.
- **(D)** $\mathrm{n}, \mathrm{m}, \mathrm{p}$ are in G.P.

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(C) $\mathrm{m}, \mathrm{n}, \mathrm{p}$ are in G.P.**

**Detailed Solution:**

For three vectors to be coplanar, their scalar triple product must be zero. Therefore, the determinant of their components is zero:

$\begin{vmatrix} m & m & n \\ 1 & 0 & 1 \\ n & n & p \end{vmatrix} = 0$

Expanding the determinant along the first row, we get:

$m(0 - n) - m(p - n) + n(n - 0) = 0$

$-mn - mp + mn + n^2 = 0$

$n^2 - mp = 0$

$n^2 = mp$

This is the condition for $m, n, p$ to be in Geometric Progression (G.P.).

Answer: $\mathrm{m}, \mathrm{n}, \mathrm{p}$ are in G.P.

</details>

---

### Q63. MHT CET 2025 (22 Apr Shift 1)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `2`

If $\bar{a}, \overline{\mathrm{~b}}, \overline{\mathrm{c}}$ are three unit vectors such that $|\bar{a}+\overline{\mathrm{b}}|^2+|\bar{a}+\overline{\mathrm{c}}|^2=8$, then $|\bar{a}+3 \overline{\mathrm{~b}}|^2+|\bar{a}+3 \overline{\mathrm{c}}|^2=$

**Options:**

- **(A)** $26$
- **(B)** $32$
- **(C)** $22$
- **(D)** $36$

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(B) $32$**

**Detailed Solution:**

Given that $\bar{a}, \bar{b}, \bar{c}$ are unit vectors, we have $|\bar{a}| = |\bar{b}| = |\bar{c}| = 1$.

The given equation is:

$|\bar{a}+\bar{b}|^2 + |\bar{a}+\bar{c}|^2 = 8$

Expanding the squares, we get:

$(|\bar{a}|^2 + |\bar{b}|^2 + 2\bar{a}\cdot\bar{b}) + (|\bar{a}|^2 + |\bar{c}|^2 + 2\bar{a}\cdot\bar{c}) = 8$

Substituting the magnitudes of the unit vectors:

$(1 + 1 + 2\bar{a}\cdot\bar{b}) + (1 + 1 + 2\bar{a}\cdot\bar{c}) = 8$

$4 + 2(\bar{a}\cdot\bar{b} + \bar{a}\cdot\bar{c}) = 8$

$2(\bar{a}\cdot\bar{b} + \bar{a}\cdot\bar{c}) = 4$

$\bar{a}\cdot\bar{b} + \bar{a}\cdot\bar{c} = 2$

We need to find the value of $|\bar{a}+3\bar{b}|^2 + |\bar{a}+3\bar{c}|^2$. Expanding this expression:

$|\bar{a}+3\bar{b}|^2 + |\bar{a}+3\bar{c}|^2 = (|\bar{a}|^2 + 9|\bar{b}|^2 + 6\bar{a}\cdot\bar{b}) + (|\bar{a}|^2 + 9|\bar{c}|^2 + 6\bar{a}\cdot\bar{c})$

Substituting the magnitudes again:

$= (1 + 9(1) + 6\bar{a}\cdot\bar{b}) + (1 + 9(1) + 6\bar{a}\cdot\bar{c})$

$= 20 + 6(\bar{a}\cdot\bar{b} + \bar{a}\cdot\bar{c})$

Substituting $\bar{a}\cdot\bar{b} + \bar{a}\cdot\bar{c} = 2$ into the expression:

$= 20 + 6(2)$

$= 20 + 12 = 32$

Answer: $32$

</details>

---

### Q64. MHT CET 2025 (21 Apr Shift 1)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `2`

The lines $\overline{\mathrm{r}}=\bar{a}+\lambda(\overline{\mathrm{b}} \times \overline{\mathrm{c}})$ and $\overline{\mathrm{r}}=\overline{\mathrm{c}}+\lambda(\bar{a} \times \overline{\mathrm{b}})$ will intersect if

**Options:**

- **(A)** $\bar{a} \times \overline{\mathrm{b}}=\overline{\mathrm{b}} \times \overline{\mathrm{c}}$
- **(B)** $\bar{a} \cdot \overline{\mathrm{~b}}=\overline{\mathrm{b}} \cdot \overline{\mathrm{c}}$
- **(C)** $\bar{a} \cdot \bar{c}=|\bar{b}|^2$
- **(D)** $\bar{a} \times \overline{\mathrm{b}}=\overline{\mathrm{c}} \times \bar{a}$

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(B) $\bar{a} \cdot \overline{\mathrm{~b}}=\overline{\mathrm{b}} \cdot \overline{\mathrm{c}}$**

**Detailed Solution:**

For two lines $\bar{r} = \bar{a}_1 + \lambda \bar{b}_1$ and $\bar{r} = \bar{a}_2 + \mu \bar{b}_2$ to intersect, they must be coplanar. The condition for coplanarity is:

$(\bar{a}_2 - \bar{a}_1) \cdot (\bar{b}_1 \times \bar{b}_2) = 0$

Given the lines $\bar{r} = \bar{a} + \lambda(\bar{b} \times \bar{c})$ and $\bar{r} = \bar{c} + \mu(\bar{a} \times \bar{b})$, we have:

$\bar{a}_1 = \bar{a}$, $\bar{b}_1 = \bar{b} \times \bar{c}$

$\bar{a}_2 = \bar{c}$, $\bar{b}_2 = \bar{a} \times \bar{b}$

Substituting these into the condition, we get:

$(\bar{c} - \bar{a}) \cdot ((\bar{b} \times \bar{c}) \times (\bar{a} \times \bar{b})) = 0$

Let us simplify the vector product $(\bar{b} \times \bar{c}) \times (\bar{a} \times \bar{b})$. Let $\bar{m} = \bar{a} \times \bar{b}$. Using the vector triple product formula:

$(\bar{b} \times \bar{c}) \times \bar{m} = (\bar{b} \cdot \bar{m})\bar{c} - (\bar{c} \cdot \bar{m})\bar{b}$

Since $\bar{m} = \bar{a} \times \bar{b}$, we know that $\bar{b} \cdot \bar{m} = \bar{b} \cdot (\bar{a} \times \bar{b}) = 0$.

Also, $\bar{c} \cdot \bar{m} = \bar{c} \cdot (\bar{a} \times \bar{b}) = [\bar{c} \quad \bar{a} \quad \bar{b}] = [\bar{a} \quad \bar{b} \quad \bar{c}]$.

Thus, $(\bar{b} \times \bar{c}) \times (\bar{a} \times \bar{b}) = -[\bar{a} \quad \bar{b} \quad \bar{c}]\bar{b}$.

Substituting this back into the coplanarity condition:

$(\bar{c} - \bar{a}) \cdot (-[\bar{a} \quad \bar{b} \quad \bar{c}]\bar{b}) = 0$

$-[\bar{a} \quad \bar{b} \quad \bar{c}] ((\bar{c} - \bar{a}) \cdot \bar{b}) = 0$

Assuming $[\bar{a} \quad \bar{b} \quad \bar{c}] \neq 0$ (the vectors are non-coplanar), we must have:

$(\bar{c} - \bar{a}) \cdot \bar{b} = 0$

$\bar{c} \cdot \bar{b} - \bar{a} \cdot \bar{b} = 0$

$\bar{a} \cdot \bar{b} = \bar{b} \cdot \bar{c}$

Answer: $\bar{a} \cdot \overline{\mathrm{~b}}=\overline{\mathrm{b}} \cdot \overline{\mathrm{c}}$

</details>

---

### Q65. MHT CET 2025 (20 Apr Shift 2)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `2`

If the points $\mathrm{A}(1,1,2), \mathrm{B}(2,1, \mathrm{p}), \mathrm{C}(1,0,3)$ and $\mathrm{D}(2,2,0)$ are coplanar then the value of $p$ is

**Options:**

- **(A)** $0$
- **(B)** $-1$
- **(C)** $1$
- **(D)** $2$

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(C) $1$**

**Detailed Solution:**

The position vectors of the points are $\vec{a} = \hat{i} + \hat{j} + 2\hat{k}$, $\vec{b} = 2\hat{i} + \hat{j} + p\hat{k}$, $\vec{c} = \hat{i} + 3\hat{k}$, and $\vec{d} = 2\hat{i} + 2\hat{j}$.

The vectors formed by these points are:

$\overrightarrow{AB} = \vec{b} - \vec{a} = \hat{i} + (p-2)\hat{k}$

$\overrightarrow{AC} = \vec{c} - \vec{a} = -\hat{j} + \hat{k}$

$\overrightarrow{AD} = \vec{d} - \vec{a} = \hat{i} + \hat{j} - 2\hat{k}$

Since the points $A, B, C, D$ are coplanar, the scalar triple product $[\overrightarrow{AB} \quad \overrightarrow{AC} \quad \overrightarrow{AD}] = 0$.

$\begin{vmatrix} 1 & 0 & p-2 \\ 0 & -1 & 1 \\ 1 & 1 & -2 \end{vmatrix} = 0$

Expanding the determinant along the first row:

$1((-1)(-2) - (1)(1)) - 0 + (p-2)(0(1) - (-1)(1)) = 0$

$1(2 - 1) + (p-2)(0 + 1) = 0$

$1 + p - 2 = 0$

$p = 1$

Answer: $1$

</details>

---

### Q66. MHT CET 2025 (20 Apr Shift 1)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `2`

The magnitude of the vector $\vec{a} \times(\vec{b} \times \vec{c})$, where $\vec{a}=\hat{i}+\hat{j}+\hat{k}$, $\vec{b}=\hat{i}+\hat{j}+2 \hat{k}$ and $\vec{c}=\hat{i}+2 \hat{j}+\hat{k}$, is

**Options:**

- **(A)** $\sqrt{2}$
- **(B)** $4 \sqrt{2}$
- **(C)** $4$
- **(D)** $2 \sqrt{3}$

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(B) $4 \sqrt{2}$**

**Detailed Solution:**

Using the vector triple product formula:

$\vec{a} \times (\vec{b} \times \vec{c}) = (\vec{a} \cdot \vec{c})\vec{b} - (\vec{a} \cdot \vec{b})\vec{c}$

First, we calculate the dot products $\vec{a} \cdot \vec{c}$ and $\vec{a} \cdot \vec{b}$:

$\vec{a} \cdot \vec{c} = (\hat{i} + \hat{j} + \hat{k}) \cdot (\hat{i} + 2\hat{j} + \hat{k}) = (1)(1) + (1)(2) + (1)(1) = 4$

$\vec{a} \cdot \vec{b} = (\hat{i} + \hat{j} + \hat{k}) \cdot (\hat{i} + \hat{j} + 2\hat{k}) = (1)(1) + (1)(1) + (1)(2) = 4$

Substituting these values into the formula, we get:

$\vec{a} \times (\vec{b} \times \vec{c}) = 4\vec{b} - 4\vec{c} = 4(\vec{b} - \vec{c})$

Now, we find the vector $\vec{b} - \vec{c}$:

$\vec{b} - \vec{c} = (\hat{i} + \hat{j} + 2\hat{k}) - (\hat{i} + 2\hat{j} + \hat{k}) = -\hat{j} + \hat{k}$

Therefore, the vector is:

$\vec{a} \times (\vec{b} \times \vec{c}) = 4(-\hat{j} + \hat{k}) = -4\hat{j} + 4\hat{k}$

The magnitude of this vector is:

$|-4\hat{j} + 4\hat{k}| = \sqrt{(-4)^2 + (4)^2} = \sqrt{16 + 16} = \sqrt{32} = 4\sqrt{2}$

Answer: $4 \sqrt{2}$

</details>

---

### Q67. MHT CET 2025 (20 Apr Shift 1)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `2`

If $\bar{a}$ and $\bar{b}$ are unit vectors and $\theta$ is the angle between them, then $\bar{a}+\bar{b}$ is a unit vector when $\theta$ is

**Options:**

- **(A)** $\frac{\pi}{3}$
- **(B)** $\frac{2 \pi}{3}$
- **(C)** $\frac{\pi}{2}$
- **(D)** $\frac{\pi}{4}$

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(B) $\frac{2 \pi}{3}$**

**Detailed Solution:**

Given $|\bar{a}| = 1$, $|\bar{b}| = 1$ and $|\bar{a}+\bar{b}| = 1$.

We know that $|\bar{a}+\bar{b}|^2 = |\bar{a}|^2 + |\bar{b}|^2 + 2|\bar{a}||\bar{b}|\cos\theta$.

Substituting the given values:

$1^2 = 1^2 + 1^2 + 2(1)(1)\cos\theta$

$1 = 2 + 2\cos\theta$

$2\cos\theta = -1$

$\cos\theta = -\dfrac{1}{2}$

$\theta = \dfrac{2\pi}{3}$

Answer: $\frac{2 \pi}{3}$

</details>

---

### Q68. MHT CET 2025 (19 Apr Shift 2)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `2`

If $\bar{a}, \bar{b}, \bar{c}$ are three vectors such that $\overline{|a|}=3, \overline{|b|}=5, \overline{|c|}=7$ then $|\bar{a}-\bar{b}|^2+|\bar{b}-\bar{c}|^2+|\bar{c}-\bar{a}|^2$ does not exceed

**Options:**

- **(A)** $83$
- **(B)** $166$
- **(C)** $249$
- **(D)** $105$

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(C) $249$**

**Detailed Solution:**

Let $S = |\bar{a}-\bar{b}|^2+|\bar{b}-\bar{c}|^2+|\bar{c}-\bar{a}|^2$.

Expanding the terms, we get:

$S = |\bar{a}|^2 - 2\bar{a}\cdot\bar{b} + |\bar{b}|^2 + |\bar{b}|^2 - 2\bar{b}\cdot\bar{c} + |\bar{c}|^2 + |\bar{c}|^2 - 2\bar{c}\cdot\bar{a} + |\bar{a}|^2$

$S = 2(|\bar{a}|^2 + |\bar{b}|^2 + |\bar{c}|^2) - 2(\bar{a}\cdot\bar{b} + \bar{b}\cdot\bar{c} + \bar{c}\cdot\bar{a})$

We know that for any three vectors, $|\bar{a}+\bar{b}+\bar{c}|^2 \ge 0$.

$|\bar{a}|^2 + |\bar{b}|^2 + |\bar{c}|^2 + 2(\bar{a}\cdot\bar{b} + \bar{b}\cdot\bar{c} + \bar{c}\cdot\bar{a}) \ge 0$

$-2(\bar{a}\cdot\bar{b} + \bar{b}\cdot\bar{c} + \bar{c}\cdot\bar{a}) \le |\bar{a}|^2 + |\bar{b}|^2 + |\bar{c}|^2$

Substituting this inequality into the expression for $S$:

$S \le 2(|\bar{a}|^2 + |\bar{b}|^2 + |\bar{c}|^2) + |\bar{a}|^2 + |\bar{b}|^2 + |\bar{c}|^2$

$S \le 3(|\bar{a}|^2 + |\bar{b}|^2 + |\bar{c}|^2)$

Given $|\bar{a}|=3$, $|\bar{b}|=5$, and $|\bar{c}|=7$:

$S \le 3(3^2 + 5^2 + 7^2)$

$S \le 3(9 + 25 + 49)$

$S \le 3(83)$

$S \le 249$

Thus, the given expression does not exceed $249$.

Answer: $249$

</details>

---

### Q69. MHT CET 2025 (19 Apr Shift 2)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `2`

In triangle ABC , the point P divides BC internally in the ratio $3: 4$ and Q divides CA internally in the ratio $5: 3$. If AP and BQ intersect in a point $G$, then $G$ divides $A P$ internally in the ratio

**Options:**

- **(A)** $2: 1$
- **(B)** $5: 7$
- **(C)** $7: 5$
- **(D)** $1: 2$

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(C) $7: 5$**

**Detailed Solution:**

Let $\vec{a}$, $\vec{b}$, and $\vec{c}$ be the position vectors of vertices $A$, $B$, and $C$ respectively.

Since $P$ divides $BC$ internally in the ratio $3:4$, the position vector of $P$ is:

$\vec{p} = \dfrac{4\vec{b} + 3\vec{c}}{3+4} = \dfrac{4\vec{b} + 3\vec{c}}{7}$

$\Rightarrow 7\vec{p} = 4\vec{b} + 3\vec{c}$

Since $Q$ divides $CA$ internally in the ratio $5:3$, the position vector of $Q$ is:

$\vec{q} = \dfrac{3\vec{c} + 5\vec{a}}{5+3} = \dfrac{3\vec{c} + 5\vec{a}}{8}$

$\Rightarrow 8\vec{q} = 3\vec{c} + 5\vec{a} \Rightarrow 3\vec{c} = 8\vec{q} - 5\vec{a}$

Substituting the value of $3\vec{c}$ into the equation for $7\vec{p}$:

$7\vec{p} = 4\vec{b} + 8\vec{q} - 5\vec{a}$

Rearranging the terms to group the vectors of the line segments $AP$ and $BQ$ together:

$7\vec{p} + 5\vec{a} = 4\vec{b} + 8\vec{q}$

Dividing both sides by $12$ (since the sum of coefficients on both sides is $7+5 = 4+8 = 12$):

$\dfrac{7\vec{p} + 5\vec{a}}{12} = \dfrac{4\vec{b} + 8\vec{q}}{12}$

$\dfrac{7\vec{p} + 5\vec{a}}{7+5} = \dfrac{\vec{b} + 2\vec{q}}{1+2}$

The left hand side represents the position vector of a point dividing the line segment $AP$ internally in the ratio $7:5$. The right hand side represents the position vector of a point dividing the line segment $BQ$ internally in the ratio $2:1$. 

Since $G$ is the point of intersection of $AP$ and $BQ$, this common position vector must be the position vector of $G$.

Therefore, $G$ divides $AP$ internally in the ratio $7:5$.

Answer: $7: 5$

</details>

---

### Q70. MHT CET 2025 (19 Apr Shift 2)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `1`

Let $\overline{\mathrm{OA}}=\overline{\mathrm{a}}, \overline{\mathrm{OB}}=\overline{\mathrm{b}}$ and if the vector along the angle bisector of $\angle \mathrm{AOB}$ is given by $x \frac{\overline{\mathrm{a}}}{|\overline{\mathrm{a}}|}+\mathrm{y} \frac{\overline{\mathrm{b}}}{|\overline{\mathrm{b}}|}$ then

**Options:**

- **(A)** $x-y=0$
- **(B)** $x+y=0$
- **(C)** $x=2 y$
- **(D)** $y=2 x$

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(A) $x-y=0$**

**Detailed Solution:**

Let $\hat{a} = \dfrac{\overline{a}}{|\overline{a}|}$ and $\hat{b} = \dfrac{\overline{b}}{|\overline{b}|}$ be the unit vectors along $\overline{OA}$ and $\overline{OB}$ respectively.

The vector along the internal angle bisector of $\angle AOB$ is proportional to the sum of the unit vectors along $\overline{OA}$ and $\overline{OB}$.

Thus, any vector along the angle bisector is of the form $\lambda(\hat{a} + \hat{b})$, where $\lambda$ is a non-zero scalar.

Given that the vector along the angle bisector is $x\hat{a} + y\hat{b}$.

Comparing the coefficients of $\hat{a}$ and $\hat{b}$, we get $x = \lambda$ and $y = \lambda$.

Therefore, $x = y$, which gives $x - y = 0$.

Answer: $x-y=0$

</details>

---

### Q71. MHT CET 2024 (16 May Shift 2)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `1`

If $\overline{\mathrm{a}}, \overline{\mathrm{b}}, \overline{\mathrm{c}}$ are three vectors such that $\overline{\mathrm{a}} \neq \overline{0}$ and $\overline{\mathrm{a}} \times \overline{\mathrm{b}}=2 \overline{\mathrm{a}} \times \overline{\mathrm{c}},|\overline{\mathrm{a}}|=|\overline{\mathrm{c}}|=1,|\overline{\mathrm{~b}}|=4$ and $|\overline{\mathrm{b}} \times \overline{\mathrm{c}}|=\sqrt{15}$. If $\bar{b}-2 \bar{c}=\lambda \bar{a}$, then $\lambda$ is

**Options:**

- **(A)** 1
- **(B)** -4
- **(C)** 3
- **(D)** -2

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(B) -4**

**Detailed Solution:**

Given $\bar{a} \times \bar{b} = 2 \bar{a} \times \bar{c}$

$\bar{a} \times (\bar{b} - 2\bar{c}) = \bar{0}$

This implies that $\bar{b} - 2\bar{c}$ is parallel to $\bar{a}$, so $\bar{b} - 2\bar{c} = \lambda \bar{a}$.

Squaring both sides, we get:

$|\bar{b} - 2\bar{c}|^2 = \lambda^2 |\bar{a}|^2$

$|\bar{b}|^2 + 4|\bar{c}|^2 - 4(\bar{b} \cdot \bar{c}) = \lambda^2 |\bar{a}|^2$

Substituting $|\bar{a}| = 1$, $|\bar{b}| = 4$, and $|\bar{c}| = 1$:

$(4)^2 + 4(1)^2 - 4(\bar{b} \cdot \bar{c}) = \lambda^2 (1)^2$

$20 - 4(\bar{b} \cdot \bar{c}) = \lambda^2$

Using Lagrange's identity for vectors $\bar{b}$ and $\bar{c}$:

$|\bar{b} \times \bar{c}|^2 + (\bar{b} \cdot \bar{c})^2 = |\bar{b}|^2 |\bar{c}|^2$

Substituting $|\bar{b} \times \bar{c}| = \sqrt{15}$:

$(\sqrt{15})^2 + (\bar{b} \cdot \bar{c})^2 = (4)^2 (1)^2$

$15 + (\bar{b} \cdot \bar{c})^2 = 16$

$(\bar{b} \cdot \bar{c})^2 = 1 \Rightarrow \bar{b} \cdot \bar{c} = \pm 1$

If $\bar{b} \cdot \bar{c} = 1$, then $\lambda^2 = 20 - 4(1) = 16 \Rightarrow \lambda = \pm 4$.

If $\bar{b} \cdot \bar{c} = -1$, then $\lambda^2 = 20 - 4(-1) = 24 \Rightarrow \lambda = \pm 2\sqrt{6}$.

From the given options, the possible value for $\lambda$ is $-4$.

Answer: -4

</details>

---

### Q72. MHT CET 2024 (16 May Shift 2)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `1`

If $\bar{a}=2 \hat{i}+2 \hat{j}+3 \hat{k}, \quad \bar{b}=-\hat{i}+2 \hat{j}+\hat{k}$ and $\bar{c}=3 \hat{i}+\hat{j}$ are the vectors such that $\bar{a}+\lambda \bar{b}$ is perpendicular to $\bar{c}$, then value of $\lambda$ is

**Options:**

- **(A)** 6
- **(B)** -6
- **(C)** 8
- **(D)** -8

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(C) 8**

**Detailed Solution:**

Given $\bar{a} = 2\hat{i} + 2\hat{j} + 3\hat{k}$, $\bar{b} = -\hat{i} + 2\hat{j} + \hat{k}$ and $\bar{c} = 3\hat{i} + \hat{j}$.

The vector $\bar{a} + \lambda \bar{b}$ is given by:

$\bar{a} + \lambda \bar{b} = (2 - \lambda)\hat{i} + (2 + 2\lambda)\hat{j} + (3 + \lambda)\hat{k}$

Since $\bar{a} + \lambda \bar{b}$ is perpendicular to $\bar{c}$, their dot product must be zero:

$(\bar{a} + \lambda \bar{b}) \cdot \bar{c} = 0$

$((2 - \lambda)\hat{i} + (2 + 2\lambda)\hat{j} + (3 + \lambda)\hat{k}) \cdot (3\hat{i} + \hat{j}) = 0$

$3(2 - \lambda) + 1(2 + 2\lambda) = 0$

$6 - 3\lambda + 2 + 2\lambda = 0$

$8 - \lambda = 0$

$\lambda = 8$

Answer: 8

</details>

---

### Q73. MHT CET 2024 (16 May Shift 2)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `1`

If $\bar{a}=2 \hat{i}-\hat{j}+\hat{k}, \bar{b}=\hat{i}+\hat{j}-2 \hat{k}$ and $\bar{c}=4 \hat{i}-2 \hat{j}+\hat{k}$, then the unit vector in the direction of $3 \bar{a}+\bar{b}-2 \bar{c}$ is

**Options:**

- **(A)** $\frac{1}{\sqrt{6}}(-\hat{i}+2 \hat{j}-\hat{k})$
- **(B)** $\frac{1}{\sqrt{6}}(\hat{i}+2 \hat{j}+\hat{k})$
- **(C)** $\frac{1}{\sqrt{6}}(2 \hat{\mathrm{i}}-\hat{\mathrm{j}}-\hat{\mathrm{k}})$
- **(D)** $\frac{1}{\sqrt{6}}(-\hat{i}-2 \hat{j}+\hat{k})$

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(A) $\frac{1}{\sqrt{6}}(-\hat{i}+2 \hat{j}-\hat{k})$**

**Detailed Solution:**

Given vectors:
$\bar{a} = 2 \hat{i} - \hat{j} + \hat{k}$
$\bar{b} = \hat{i} + \hat{j} - 2 \hat{k}$
$\bar{c} = 4 \hat{i} - 2 \hat{j} + \hat{k}$

Calculate $3 \bar{a} + \bar{b} - 2 \bar{c}$:
$3 \bar{a} = 3(2 \hat{i} - \hat{j} + \hat{k}) = 6 \hat{i} - 3 \hat{j} + 3 \hat{k}$
$\bar{b} = \hat{i} + \hat{j} - 2 \hat{k}$
$-2 \bar{c} = -2(4 \hat{i} - 2 \hat{j} + \hat{k}) = -8 \hat{i} + 4 \hat{j} - 2 \hat{k}$

Adding the components together:
$3 \bar{a} + \bar{b} - 2 \bar{c} = (6 + 1 - 8) \hat{i} + (-3 + 1 + 4) \hat{j} + (3 - 2 - 2) \hat{k}$
$3 \bar{a} + \bar{b} - 2 \bar{c} = -\hat{i} + 2 \hat{j} - \hat{k}$

Magnitude of the resulting vector:
$|3 \bar{a} + \bar{b} - 2 \bar{c}| = \sqrt{(-1)^2 + (2)^2 + (-1)^2} = \sqrt{1 + 4 + 1} = \sqrt{6}$

The unit vector in the direction of $3 \bar{a} + \bar{b} - 2 \bar{c}$ is:
$\dfrac{1}{\sqrt{6}}(-\hat{i} + 2 \hat{j} - \hat{k})$

Answer: $\frac{1}{\sqrt{6}}(-\hat{i}+2 \hat{j}-\hat{k})$

</details>

---

### Q74. MHT CET 2024 (16 May Shift 1)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `2`

If the vectors $\overline{\mathrm{AB}}=3 \hat{\mathrm{i}}+4 \hat{\mathrm{k}}$ and $\overline{\mathrm{AC}}=5 \hat{\mathrm{i}}-2 \hat{\mathrm{j}}+4 \hat{\mathrm{k}}$ are the sides of the triangle ABC , then the length of the median, through $A$, is

**Options:**

- **(A)** $\sqrt{45}$ units.
- **(B)** $\sqrt{18}$ units.
- **(C)** $\sqrt{72}$ units.
- **(D)** $\sqrt{33}$ units

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(D) $\sqrt{33}$ units**

**Detailed Solution:**

Let $\overline{\mathrm{AM}}$ be the median through the vertex A of the triangle ABC.

The vector $\overline{\mathrm{AM}}$ is given by $\dfrac{\overline{\mathrm{AB}} + \overline{\mathrm{AC}}}{2}$.

Substituting the given vectors:

$\overline{\mathrm{AB}} + \overline{\mathrm{AC}} = (3\hat{i} + 4\hat{k}) + (5\hat{i} - 2\hat{j} + 4\hat{k}) = 8\hat{i} - 2\hat{j} + 8\hat{k}$

$\overline{\mathrm{AM}} = \dfrac{8\hat{i} - 2\hat{j} + 8\hat{k}}{2} = 4\hat{i} - \hat{j} + 4\hat{k}$

The length of the median is the magnitude of $\overline{\mathrm{AM}}$:

$|\overline{\mathrm{AM}}| = \sqrt{4^2 + (-1)^2 + 4^2} = \sqrt{16 + 1 + 16} = \sqrt{33}$ units.

Answer: $\sqrt{33}$ units

</details>

---

### Q75. MHT CET 2024 (15 May Shift 2)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `2`

One side and one diagonal of a parallelogram are represented by $3 \hat{i}+\hat{j}-\hat{k}$ and $2 \hat{i}+\hat{j}-2 \hat{k}$ respectively, then the area of parallelogram in square units is

**Options:**

- **(A)** $2 \sqrt{3}$
- **(B)** $3 \sqrt{2}$
- **(C)** $6 \sqrt{2}$
- **(D)** $4 \sqrt{3}$

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(B) $3 \sqrt{2}$**

**Detailed Solution:**

Let the given side of the parallelogram be $\vec{a} = 3\hat{i} + \hat{j} - \hat{k}$ and the given diagonal be $\vec{d} = 2\hat{i} + \hat{j} - 2\hat{k}$.

Let the other side of the parallelogram be $\vec{b}$. The diagonal of the parallelogram can be represented as $\vec{d} = \vec{a} + \vec{b}$ (or $\vec{a} - \vec{b}$).

The area of the parallelogram is given by the magnitude of the cross product of its adjacent sides, which is $|\vec{a} \times \vec{b}|$.

Since $\vec{b} = \vec{d} - \vec{a}$, we have:

$\vec{a} \times \vec{b} = \vec{a} \times (\vec{d} - \vec{a}) = \vec{a} \times \vec{d} - \vec{a} \times \vec{a}$

Since $\vec{a} \times \vec{a} = 0$, we get:

$\vec{a} \times \vec{b} = \vec{a} \times \vec{d}$

Now, calculating the cross product $\vec{a} \times \vec{d}$:

$\vec{a} \times \vec{d} = \begin{vmatrix} \hat{i} & \hat{j} & \hat{k} \\ 3 & 1 & -1 \\ 2 & 1 & -2 \end{vmatrix}$

$\vec{a} \times \vec{d} = \hat{i}(-2 - (-1)) - \hat{j}(-6 - (-2)) + \hat{k}(3 - 2)$

$\vec{a} \times \vec{d} = -\hat{i} + 4\hat{j} + \hat{k}$

The area of the parallelogram is the magnitude of this vector:

Area $= |\vec{a} \times \vec{d}| = \sqrt{(-1)^2 + (4)^2 + (1)^2}$

Area $= \sqrt{1 + 16 + 1} = \sqrt{18} = 3\sqrt{2}$ square units.

Answer: $3 \sqrt{2}$

</details>

---

### Q76. MHT CET 2024 (15 May Shift 2)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `1`

If the points $P, Q$ and $R$ are with the position vectors $\hat{i}-2 \hat{j}+3 \hat{k},-2 \hat{i}+3 \hat{j}+2 \hat{k}$ and $-8 \hat{i}+13 \hat{j}$ respectively, then these points are

**Options:**

- **(A)** collinear and Q lies between P and R .
- **(B)** collinear and R lies between P and Q .
- **(C)** collinear and P lies between Q and R .
- **(D)** non-collinear.

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(A) collinear and Q lies between P and R .**

**Detailed Solution:**

Let the position vectors of the points be $\vec{p} = \hat{i} - 2\hat{j} + 3\hat{k}$, $\vec{q} = -2\hat{i} + 3\hat{j} + 2\hat{k}$, and $\vec{r} = -8\hat{i} + 13\hat{j}$.

We find the vectors $\overrightarrow{PQ}$ and $\overrightarrow{QR}$:
$\overrightarrow{PQ} = \vec{q} - \vec{p} = (-2 - 1)\hat{i} + (3 - (-2))\hat{j} + (2 - 3)\hat{k} = -3\hat{i} + 5\hat{j} - \hat{k}$

$\overrightarrow{QR} = \vec{r} - \vec{q} = (-8 - (-2))\hat{i} + (13 - 3)\hat{j} + (0 - 2)\hat{k} = -6\hat{i} + 10\hat{j} - 2\hat{k}$

Observing the two vectors, we get:
$\overrightarrow{QR} = 2(-3\hat{i} + 5\hat{j} - \hat{k}) = 2\overrightarrow{PQ}$

Since $\overrightarrow{QR}$ is a scalar multiple of $\overrightarrow{PQ}$ and they share the common point $Q$, the points $P, Q$, and $R$ are collinear.

Further, expanding the relation:
$\vec{r} - \vec{q} = 2(\vec{q} - \vec{p})$

$\vec{r} - \vec{q} = 2\vec{q} - 2\vec{p}$

$3\vec{q} = 2\vec{p} + \vec{r}$

$\vec{q} = \dfrac{2\vec{p} + 1\vec{r}}{2 + 1}$

This indicates that the point $Q$ divides the line segment joining $P$ and $R$ internally in the ratio $1:2$. Since the division is internal, $Q$ lies between $P$ and $R$.

Answer: collinear and Q lies between P and R .

</details>

---

### Q77. MHT CET 2024 (15 May Shift 2)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `2`

Let $P, Q, R$ and $S$ be the points on the plane with position vectors $-2 \hat{i}-\hat{j}, 4 \hat{i}, 3 \hat{i}+3 \hat{j}$ and $-3 \hat{i}+2 \hat{j}$ respectively. Then the quadrilateral PQRS must be a

**Options:**

- **(A)** parallelogram, which is neither a rhombus nor a rectangle.
- **(B)** square.
- **(C)** rectangle, but not a square.
- **(D)** rhombus, but not a square.

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(A) parallelogram, which is neither a rhombus nor a rectangle.**

**Detailed Solution:**

Let the position vectors of the points be $\vec{p} = -2\hat{i} - \hat{j}$, $\vec{q} = 4\hat{i}$, $\vec{r} = 3\hat{i} + 3\hat{j}$, and $\vec{s} = -3\hat{i} + 2\hat{j}$.

The vectors representing the sides of the quadrilateral are:
$\overrightarrow{PQ} = \vec{q} - \vec{p} = (4 - (-2))\hat{i} + (0 - (-1))\hat{j} = 6\hat{i} + \hat{j}$
$\overrightarrow{QR} = \vec{r} - \vec{q} = (3 - 4)\hat{i} + (3 - 0)\hat{j} = -\hat{i} + 3\hat{j}$
$\overrightarrow{RS} = \vec{s} - \vec{r} = (-3 - 3)\hat{i} + (2 - 3)\hat{j} = -6\hat{i} - \hat{j}$
$\overrightarrow{SP} = \vec{p} - \vec{s} = (-2 - (-3))\hat{i} + (-1 - 2)\hat{j} = \hat{i} - 3\hat{j}$

We observe that $\overrightarrow{RS} = -\overrightarrow{PQ}$ and $\overrightarrow{SP} = -\overrightarrow{QR}$. This implies that opposite sides are parallel and equal in length, so PQRS is a parallelogram.

The lengths of the adjacent sides are:
$|\overrightarrow{PQ}| = \sqrt{6^2 + 1^2} = \sqrt{37}$
$|\overrightarrow{QR}| = \sqrt{(-1)^2 + 3^2} = \sqrt{10}$
Since $|\overrightarrow{PQ}| \neq |\overrightarrow{QR}|$, the adjacent sides are not equal, meaning PQRS is not a rhombus.

Now, checking the angle between adjacent sides:
$\overrightarrow{PQ} \cdot \overrightarrow{QR} = (6)(-1) + (1)(3) = -6 + 3 = -3$
Since the dot product is not zero, the adjacent sides are not perpendicular, meaning PQRS is not a rectangle.

Therefore, PQRS is a parallelogram, which is neither a rhombus nor a rectangle.

Answer: parallelogram, which is neither a rhombus nor a rectangle.

</details>

---

### Q78. MHT CET 2024 (11 May Shift 2)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `1`

Let $\overline{\mathrm{a}}, \overline{\mathrm{b}}$, and $\overline{\mathrm{c}}$ be three non-zero vectors such that no two of these are collinear. If the vector $\overline{\mathrm{a}}+2 \overline{\mathrm{~b}}$ is collinear with $\overline{\mathrm{c}}$ and $\overline{\mathrm{b}}+3 \overline{\mathrm{c}}$ is collinear with $\overline{\mathrm{a}}$, then $\overline{\mathrm{a}}+2 \overline{\mathrm{~b}}+6 \overline{\mathrm{c}}$ equals

**Options:**

- **(A)** $\quad \lambda \bar{c}$ ( $\lambda$ being some non-zero scalar)
- **(B)** $\lambda \bar{b}$ ( $\lambda$ being some non-zero scalar)
- **(C)** $\lambda \bar{a}$ ( $\lambda$ being some non-zero scalar)
- **(D)** $\overline{0}$ ( $\lambda$ being some non-zero scalar)

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(D) $\overline{0}$ ( $\lambda$ being some non-zero scalar)**

**Detailed Solution:**

Since $\overline{a} + 2\overline{b}$ is collinear with $\overline{c}$, there exists a scalar $\lambda$ such that:

$\overline{a} + 2\overline{b} = \lambda \overline{c}$

Adding $6\overline{c}$ to both sides, we get:

$\overline{a} + 2\overline{b} + 6\overline{c} = (\lambda + 6)\overline{c}$

Since $\overline{b} + 3\overline{c}$ is collinear with $\overline{a}$, there exists a scalar $\mu$ such that:

$\overline{b} + 3\overline{c} = \mu \overline{a}$

Multiplying by $2$ and adding $\overline{a}$ to both sides, we get:

$\overline{a} + 2\overline{b} + 6\overline{c} = (2\mu + 1)\overline{a}$

Equating the two expressions for $\overline{a} + 2\overline{b} + 6\overline{c}$, we obtain:

$(\lambda + 6)\overline{c} = (2\mu + 1)\overline{a}$

Since $\overline{a}$ and $\overline{c}$ are non-zero and non-collinear vectors, the only way this equation can hold is if the coefficients of both vectors are zero. Thus:

$\lambda + 6 = 0 \Rightarrow \lambda = -6$

$2\mu + 1 = 0 \Rightarrow \mu = -\dfrac{1}{2}$

Substituting $\lambda = -6$ into the first expression, we get:

$\overline{a} + 2\overline{b} + 6\overline{c} = (-6 + 6)\overline{c} = \overline{0}$

Answer: $\overline{0}$ ( $\lambda$ being some non-zero scalar)

</details>

---

### Q79. MHT CET 2024 (11 May Shift 1)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `3`

If $\bar{x}=\frac{\overline{\mathrm{b}} \times \overline{\mathrm{c}}}{[\overline{\mathrm{a}} \overline{\mathrm{b}} \overline{\mathrm{c}}]}, \bar{y}=\frac{\overline{\mathrm{c}} \times \overline{\mathrm{a}}}{[\overline{\mathrm{a}} \overline{\mathrm{b}} \overline{\mathrm{c}}]}$ and $\overline{\mathrm{z}}=\frac{\overline{\mathrm{a}} \times \overline{\mathrm{b}}}{[\overline{\mathrm{a}} \overline{\mathrm{b}} \overline{\mathrm{c}}]}$ where $\overline{\mathrm{a}}, \overline{\mathrm{b}}, \overline{\mathrm{c}}$ are non-coplanar vectors, then value of $\bar{x} \cdot(\overline{\mathrm{a}}+\overline{\mathrm{b}})+\bar{y} \cdot(\overline{\mathrm{~b}}+\overline{\mathrm{c}})+\overline{\mathrm{z}} \cdot(\overline{\mathrm{c}}+\overline{\mathrm{a}})$ is

**Options:**

- **(A)** 3
- **(B)** 1
- **(C)** -1
- **(D)** 0

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(A) 3**

**Detailed Solution:**

The given vectors $\overline{x}, \overline{y}, \overline{z}$ are the reciprocal vectors of $\overline{a}, \overline{b}, \overline{c}$.

Using the properties of scalar triple product, we have:

$\overline{x} \cdot \overline{a} = \dfrac{(\overline{b} \times \overline{c}) \cdot \overline{a}}{[\overline{a} \overline{b} \overline{c}]} = \dfrac{[\overline{b} \overline{c} \overline{a}]}{[\overline{a} \overline{b} \overline{c}]} = 1$

$\overline{x} \cdot \overline{b} = \dfrac{(\overline{b} \times \overline{c}) \cdot \overline{b}}{[\overline{a} \overline{b} \overline{c}]} = 0$

Similarly, we get:

$\overline{y} \cdot \overline{b} = 1$, $\overline{y} \cdot \overline{c} = 0$

$\overline{z} \cdot \overline{c} = 1$, $\overline{z} \cdot \overline{a} = 0$

The given expression is:

$\overline{x} \cdot (\overline{a} + \overline{b}) + \overline{y} \cdot (\overline{b} + \overline{c}) + \overline{z} \cdot (\overline{c} + \overline{a})$

Expanding the dot products:

$= \overline{x} \cdot \overline{a} + \overline{x} \cdot \overline{b} + \overline{y} \cdot \overline{b} + \overline{y} \cdot \overline{c} + \overline{z} \cdot \overline{c} + \overline{z} \cdot \overline{a}$

Substituting the values:

$= 1 + 0 + 1 + 0 + 1 + 0 = 3$

Answer: 3

</details>

---

### Q80. MHT CET 2024 (11 May Shift 1)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `2`

Let $\overline{\mathrm{a}}, \overline{\mathrm{b}}$ and $\overline{\mathrm{c}}$ be three non-zero vectors such that no two of them are collinear and $(\overline{\mathrm{a}} \times \overline{\mathrm{b}}) \times \overline{\mathrm{c}}=\frac{1}{3}|\overline{\mathrm{~b}}||\overline{\mathrm{c}}| \overline{\mathrm{a}}$. If ' $\theta$ ' is the angle between the vectors $\overline{\mathrm{b}}$ and $\overline{\mathrm{c}}$, then value of $\sin \theta$ is

**Options:**

- **(A)** $\frac{2}{3}$
- **(B)** $\frac{-\sqrt{2}}{3}$
- **(C)** $-\frac{1}{3}$
- **(D)** $\frac{2 \sqrt{2}}{3}$

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(D) $\frac{2 \sqrt{2}}{3}$**

**Detailed Solution:**

Using the vector triple product expansion:

$(\vec{a} \times \vec{b}) \times \vec{c} = (\vec{a} \cdot \vec{c})\vec{b} - (\vec{b} \cdot \vec{c})\vec{a}$

Given the equation:

$(\vec{a} \times \vec{b}) \times \vec{c} = \dfrac{1}{3}|\vec{b}||\vec{c}|\vec{a}$

Equating the two expressions:

$(\vec{a} \cdot \vec{c})\vec{b} - (\vec{b} \cdot \vec{c})\vec{a} = \dfrac{1}{3}|\vec{b}||\vec{c}|\vec{a}$

Since $\vec{a}$ and $\vec{b}$ are non-collinear vectors, their coefficients on both sides must be equal. Comparing the coefficients of $\vec{a}$:

$-(\vec{b} \cdot \vec{c}) = \dfrac{1}{3}|\vec{b}||\vec{c}|$

Using the dot product formula $\vec{b} \cdot \vec{c} = |\vec{b}||\vec{c}|\cos\theta$, where $\theta$ is the angle between $\vec{b}$ and $\vec{c}$:

$-|\vec{b}||\vec{c}|\cos\theta = \dfrac{1}{3}|\vec{b}||\vec{c}|$

Since $\vec{b}$ and $\vec{c}$ are non-zero vectors, $|\vec{b}||\vec{c}| \neq 0$. Dividing both sides by $-|\vec{b}||\vec{c}|$:

$\cos\theta = -\dfrac{1}{3}$

Since $\theta$ is the angle between two vectors, $0 \le \theta \le \pi$, which implies $\sin\theta \ge 0$. Using the trigonometric identity $\sin^2\theta + \cos^2\theta = 1$:

$\sin\theta = \sqrt{1 - \cos^2\theta} = \sqrt{1 - \left(-\dfrac{1}{3}\right)^2}$

$\sin\theta = \sqrt{1 - \dfrac{1}{9}} = \sqrt{\dfrac{8}{9}} = \dfrac{2\sqrt{2}}{3}$

Answer: $\frac{2 \sqrt{2}}{3}$

</details>

---

### Q81. MHT CET 2024 (10 May Shift 2)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `1`

$\bar{a}=\hat{i}+\hat{j}+\hat{k}, \bar{b}=4 \hat{i}-2 \hat{j}+3 \hat{k}, \bar{c}=\hat{i}-2 \hat{j}+\hat{k}$, then $a$ vector of magnitude 6 units, which is parallel to the vector $2 \bar{a}-\bar{b}+3 c$, is

**Options:**

- **(A)** $2 \hat{i}-4 \hat{j}+4 \hat{k}$
- **(B)** $\hat{i}-\hat{j}+2 \hat{k}$
- **(C)** $4 \hat{i}+4 \hat{j}-2 \hat{k}$
- **(D)** $2 \hat{\mathrm{i}}+4 \hat{\mathrm{j}}+4 \hat{\mathrm{k}}$

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(A) $2 \hat{i}-4 \hat{j}+4 \hat{k}$**

**Detailed Solution:**

Given vectors are:
$\bar{a} = \hat{i} + \hat{j} + \hat{k}$
$\bar{b} = 4\hat{i} - 2\hat{j} + 3\hat{k}$
$\bar{c} = \hat{i} - 2\hat{j} + \hat{k}$

Let $\bar{v} = 2\bar{a} - \bar{b} + 3\bar{c}$. Substituting the given vectors:
$\bar{v} = 2(\hat{i} + \hat{j} + \hat{k}) - (4\hat{i} - 2\hat{j} + 3\hat{k}) + 3(\hat{i} - 2\hat{j} + \hat{k})$

$\bar{v} = (2 - 4 + 3)\hat{i} + (2 + 2 - 6)\hat{j} + (2 - 3 + 3)\hat{k}$

$\bar{v} = \hat{i} - 2\hat{j} + 2\hat{k}$

The magnitude of $\bar{v}$ is:
$|\bar{v}| = \sqrt{1^2 + (-2)^2 + 2^2} = \sqrt{1 + 4 + 4} = \sqrt{9} = 3$

The unit vector in the direction of $\bar{v}$ is:
$\hat{v} = \dfrac{\bar{v}}{|\bar{v}|} = \dfrac{\hat{i} - 2\hat{j} + 2\hat{k}}{3}$

A vector of magnitude 6 units parallel to $\bar{v}$ is:
$6\hat{v} = 6 \left( \dfrac{\hat{i} - 2\hat{j} + 2\hat{k}}{3} \right) = 2(\hat{i} - 2\hat{j} + 2\hat{k}) = 2\hat{i} - 4\hat{j} + 4\hat{k}$

Answer: $2 \hat{i}-4 \hat{j}+4 \hat{k}$

</details>

---

### Q82. MHT CET 2024 (10 May Shift 2)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `3`

Let two non-collinear unit vectors $\hat{a}$ and $\hat{b}$ form an acute angle. A point P moves, so that at any time $t$ the position vector $\overline{\mathrm{OP}}$, where O is the origin, is given by $\hat{a} \cos t+\hat{b} \sin t$. When $P$ is farthest from origin O , let M be the length of $\overline{\mathrm{OP}}$ and $\hat{\mathrm{u}}$ be the unit vector along $\overline{\mathrm{OP}}$, then

**Options:**

- **(A)** $\hat{\mathrm{u}}=\dfrac{\hat{\mathrm{a}}+\hat{\mathrm{b}}}{|\hat{\mathrm{a}}+\hat{\mathrm{b}}|}$ and $\mathrm{M}=(1+\hat{\mathrm{a}} \cdot \hat{\mathrm{b}})^{\dfrac{1}{2}}$
- **(B)** $\hat{\mathrm{u}}=\dfrac{\hat{\mathrm{a}}-\hat{\mathrm{b}}}{|\hat{\mathrm{a}}-\hat{\mathrm{b}}|}$ and $\mathrm{M}=(1+\hat{\mathrm{a}} \cdot \hat{\mathrm{b}})^{\dfrac{1}{2}}$
- **(C)** $\quad \dot{\hat{u}}=\dfrac{\hat{a}+\hat{b}}{|\hat{a}+\hat{b}|}$ and $M=(1+2 \hat{a} \cdot \hat{b})^{\dfrac{1}{2}}$
- **(D)** $\quad \hat{\mathrm{u}}=\dfrac{\hat{\mathrm{a}}-\hat{\mathrm{b}}}{|\hat{\mathrm{a}}-\hat{\mathrm{b}}|}$ and $\mathrm{M}=(1-2 \hat{\mathrm{a}} \cdot \hat{\mathrm{b}})^{\dfrac{1}{2}}$

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(A) $\hat{\mathrm{u}}=\dfrac{\hat{\mathrm{a}}+\hat{\mathrm{b}}}{|\hat{\mathrm{a}}+\hat{\mathrm{b}}|}$ and $\mathrm{M}=(1+\hat{\mathrm{a}} \cdot \hat{\mathrm{b}})^{\dfrac{1}{2}}$**

**Detailed Solution:**

The position vector of point P is given by $\vec{r} = \hat{a} \cos t + \hat{b} \sin t$.

The square of the distance from the origin is:

$|\vec{r}|^2 = (\hat{a} \cos t + \hat{b} \sin t) \cdot (\hat{a} \cos t + \hat{b} \sin t)$

$|\vec{r}|^2 = |\hat{a}|^2 \cos^2 t + |\hat{b}|^2 \sin^2 t + 2(\hat{a} \cdot \hat{b}) \sin t \cos t$

Since $\hat{a}$ and $\hat{b}$ are unit vectors, $|\hat{a}| = 1$ and $|\hat{b}| = 1$.

$|\vec{r}|^2 = \cos^2 t + \sin^2 t + (\hat{a} \cdot \hat{b}) \sin 2t$

$|\vec{r}|^2 = 1 + (\hat{a} \cdot \hat{b}) \sin 2t$

Since $\hat{a}$ and $\hat{b}$ form an acute angle, $\hat{a} \cdot \hat{b} > 0$. To maximize $|\vec{r}|^2$, we must have $\sin 2t = 1$, which gives $2t = \dfrac{\pi}{2} \Rightarrow t = \dfrac{\pi}{4}$.

The maximum length $M$ is:

$M = (1 + \hat{a} \cdot \hat{b})^{\dfrac{1}{2}}$

At $t = \dfrac{\pi}{4}$, the position vector is:

$\vec{r} = \hat{a} \cos\left(\dfrac{\pi}{4}\right) + \hat{b} \sin\left(\dfrac{\pi}{4}\right) = \dfrac{1}{\sqrt{2}}(\hat{a} + \hat{b})$

The unit vector $\hat{u}$ along $\vec{r}$ is:

$\hat{u} = \dfrac{\vec{r}}{|\vec{r}|} = \dfrac{\dfrac{1}{\sqrt{2}}(\hat{a} + \hat{b})}{\left|\dfrac{1}{\sqrt{2}}(\hat{a} + \hat{b})\right|} = \dfrac{\hat{a} + \hat{b}}{|\hat{a} + \hat{b}|}$

Answer: $\hat{\mathrm{u}}=\dfrac{\hat{\mathrm{a}}+\hat{\mathrm{b}}}{|\hat{\mathrm{a}}+\hat{\mathrm{b}}|}$ and $\mathrm{M}=(1+\hat{\mathrm{a}} \cdot \hat{\mathrm{b}})^{\dfrac{1}{2}}$

</details>

---

### Q83. MHT CET 2024 (10 May Shift 1)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `2`

Let $\overline{\mathrm{a}}, \overline{\mathrm{b}}$ and $\overline{\mathrm{c}}$ be three non-zero vectors such that no two of them are collinear and $(\overline{\mathrm{a}} \times \overline{\mathrm{b}}) \times \overline{\mathrm{c}}=\dfrac{1}{3}|\overline{\mathrm{~b}}||\overline{\mathrm{c}}| \overline{\mathrm{a}}$. If $\theta$ is the angle between vectors $\overline{\mathrm{b}}$ and $\overline{\mathrm{c}}$, then the value of $\operatorname{cosec} \theta$ is

**Options:**

- **(A)** $\dfrac{3 \sqrt{3}}{2}$
- **(B)** $\dfrac{2 \sqrt{2}}{3}$
- **(C)** $\dfrac{2}{\sqrt{3}}$
- **(D)** $\dfrac{3}{2 \sqrt{2}}$

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(D) $\dfrac{3}{2 \sqrt{2}}$**

**Detailed Solution:**

Given equation is $(\overline{\mathrm{a}} \times \overline{\mathrm{b}}) \times \overline{\mathrm{c}} = \dfrac{1}{3}|\overline{\mathrm{b}}||\overline{\mathrm{c}}| \overline{\mathrm{a}}$

Using the vector triple product expansion, we have:

$(\overline{\mathrm{a}} \cdot \overline{\mathrm{c}}) \overline{\mathrm{b}} - (\overline{\mathrm{b}} \cdot \overline{\mathrm{c}}) \overline{\mathrm{a}} = \dfrac{1}{3}|\overline{\mathrm{b}}||\overline{\mathrm{c}}| \overline{\mathrm{a}}$

Since $\overline{\mathrm{a}}$ and $\overline{\mathrm{b}}$ are non-collinear vectors, their coefficients on both sides must be equal. Comparing the coefficients of $\overline{\mathrm{a}}$ and $\overline{\mathrm{b}}$, we get:

$\overline{\mathrm{a}} \cdot \overline{\mathrm{c}} = 0$

$-(\overline{\mathrm{b}} \cdot \overline{\mathrm{c}}) = \dfrac{1}{3}|\overline{\mathrm{b}}||\overline{\mathrm{c}}|$

Using the dot product formula $\overline{\mathrm{b}} \cdot \overline{\mathrm{c}} = |\overline{\mathrm{b}}||\overline{\mathrm{c}}| \cos \theta$, where $\theta$ is the angle between $\overline{\mathrm{b}}$ and $\overline{\mathrm{c}}$:

$-|\overline{\mathrm{b}}||\overline{\mathrm{c}}| \cos \theta = \dfrac{1}{3}|\overline{\mathrm{b}}||\overline{\mathrm{c}}|$

Since $\overline{\mathrm{b}}$ and $\overline{\mathrm{c}}$ are non-zero vectors, dividing by $|\overline{\mathrm{b}}||\overline{\mathrm{c}}|$ gives:

$\cos \theta = -\dfrac{1}{3}$

Since $\theta$ is the angle between two vectors, $0 \leq \theta \leq \pi$. Thus, $\sin \theta$ is positive.

$\sin \theta = \sqrt{1 - \cos^2 \theta} = \sqrt{1 - \left(-\dfrac{1}{3}\right)^2} = \sqrt{1 - \dfrac{1}{9}} = \dfrac{2\sqrt{2}}{3}$

Therefore, $\operatorname{cosec} \theta = \dfrac{1}{\sin \theta} = \dfrac{3}{2\sqrt{2}}$

Answer: $\dfrac{3}{2 \sqrt{2}}$

</details>

---

### Q84. MHT CET 2024 (09 May Shift 2)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `2`

A unit vector coplanar with $\hat{i}+\hat{j}+\hat{k}$ and $2 \hat{i}+\hat{j}+\hat{k}$ and perpendicular to $\hat{i}+\hat{j}-\hat{k}$ is

**Options:**

- **(A)** $+\dfrac{1}{\sqrt{2}}(-\hat{j}-\hat{k})$
- **(B)** $\dfrac{(\hat{\mathrm{j}}-\hat{\mathrm{k}})}{\sqrt{2}}$
- **(C)** $\dfrac{-\hat{\mathrm{j}}+2 \hat{\mathrm{k}}}{\sqrt{5}}$
- **(D)** $+\dfrac{1}{\sqrt{26}}(\hat{\mathrm{j}}+5 \hat{\mathrm{k}})$

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(A) $+\dfrac{1}{\sqrt{2}}(-\hat{j}-\hat{k})$**

**Detailed Solution:**

Let $\vec{a} = \hat{i}+\hat{j}+\hat{k}$, $\vec{b} = 2\hat{i}+\hat{j}+\hat{k}$, and $\vec{c} = \hat{i}+\hat{j}-\hat{k}$.

The required vector is coplanar with $\vec{a}$ and $\vec{b}$, which means it lies in the plane containing $\vec{a}$ and $\vec{b}$. Therefore, it is perpendicular to the normal vector of this plane, which is $\vec{a} \times \vec{b}$.

The required vector is also given to be perpendicular to $\vec{c}$.

Thus, the required vector is parallel to the cross product of $(\vec{a} \times \vec{b})$ and $\vec{c}$.

First, we find $\vec{a} \times \vec{b}$:
$\vec{a} \times \vec{b} = \begin{vmatrix} \hat{i} & \hat{j} & \hat{k} \\ 1 & 1 & 1 \\ 2 & 1 & 1 \end{vmatrix} = \hat{i}(1-1) - \hat{j}(1-2) + \hat{k}(1-2) = \hat{j} - \hat{k}$

Now, we find the vector parallel to $(\vec{a} \times \vec{b}) \times \vec{c}$:
$(\vec{a} \times \vec{b}) \times \vec{c} = (\hat{j} - \hat{k}) \times (\hat{i} + \hat{j} - \hat{k}) = \begin{vmatrix} \hat{i} & \hat{j} & \hat{k} \\ 0 & 1 & -1 \\ 1 & 1 & -1 \end{vmatrix}$

$= \hat{i}(-1 - (-1)) - \hat{j}(0 - (-1)) + \hat{k}(0 - 1)$

$= -\hat{j} - \hat{k}$

The required unit vector is obtained by dividing this vector by its magnitude:
$\hat{v} = \pm \dfrac{-\hat{j}-\hat{k}}{\sqrt{(-1)^2 + (-1)^2}} = \pm \dfrac{1}{\sqrt{2}}(-\hat{j}-\hat{k})$

Comparing with the given options, the correct unit vector is $+\dfrac{1}{\sqrt{2}}(-\hat{j}-\hat{k})$.

Answer: $+\dfrac{1}{\sqrt{2}}(-\hat{j}-\hat{k})$

</details>

---

### Q85. MHT CET 2024 (09 May Shift 2)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `1`

The vector $\overline{\mathrm{a}}=\alpha \hat{\mathrm{i}}+2 \hat{\mathrm{j}}+\beta \hat{\mathrm{k}}$ lies in the plane of the vectors $\bar{b}=\hat{i}+\hat{j}$ and $\bar{c}=\hat{j}+\hat{k}$ and bisects the angle between $\bar{b}$ and $\bar{c}$. Then which one of the following gives possible values of $\alpha$ and $\beta$ ?

**Options:**

- **(A)** $\alpha=1, \beta=1$
- **(B)** $\alpha=2, \beta=2$
- **(C)** $\alpha=1, \beta=2$
- **(D)** $\alpha=2, \beta=1$

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(A) $\alpha=1, \beta=1$**

**Detailed Solution:**

Given $\vec{b} = \hat{i} + \hat{j}$ and $\vec{c} = \hat{j} + \hat{k}$.

The magnitudes are $|\vec{b}| = \sqrt{1^2 + 1^2} = \sqrt{2}$ and $|\vec{c}| = \sqrt{1^2 + 1^2} = \sqrt{2}$.

Since $|\vec{b}| = |\vec{c}|$, the vectors bisecting the angle between $\vec{b}$ and $\vec{c}$ are parallel to $\vec{b} + \vec{c}$ and $\vec{b} - \vec{c}$.

$\vec{b} + \vec{c} = \hat{i} + 2\hat{j} + \hat{k}$

$\vec{b} - \vec{c} = \hat{i} - \hat{k}$

The vector $\vec{a}$ is given as $\alpha \hat{i} + 2 \hat{j} + \beta \hat{k}$. Since the $\hat{j}$ component of $\vec{a}$ is non-zero, $\vec{a}$ must be parallel to $\vec{b} + \vec{c}$.

$\vec{a} = \lambda (\vec{b} + \vec{c}) = \lambda (\hat{i} + 2\hat{j} + \hat{k})$

Comparing the $\hat{j}$ components, we get $2\lambda = 2 \Rightarrow \lambda = 1$.

Substituting $\lambda = 1$, we get $\vec{a} = \hat{i} + 2\hat{j} + \hat{k}$.

Comparing this with $\vec{a} = \alpha \hat{i} + 2 \hat{j} + \beta \hat{k}$, we get $\alpha = 1$ and $\beta = 1$.

Answer: $\alpha=1, \beta=1$

</details>

---

### Q86. MHT CET 2024 (09 May Shift 1)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `2`

Suppose that $\bar{p}, \bar{q}$ and $\bar{r}$ are three non-coplanar vectors in $\mathbb{R}^3$. Let the components of a vector $\bar{s}$ along $\bar{p}, \bar{q}$ and $\bar{r}$ be 4,3 and 5 respectively. If the components of this vector $\overline{\mathrm{s}}$ along $(-\overline{\mathrm{p}}+\overline{\mathrm{q}}+\overline{\mathrm{r}}),(\overline{\mathrm{p}}-\overline{\mathrm{q}}+\overline{\mathrm{r}})$ and $(-\overline{\mathrm{p}}-\overline{\mathrm{q}}+\overline{\mathrm{r}})$ are $x$, $y$ and $z$ respectively, then the value of $2 x+y+z$ is

**Options:**

- **(A)** 10
- **(B)** 6
- **(C)** 9
- **(D)** 8

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(C) 9**

**Detailed Solution:**

Given that the components of $\bar{s}$ along $\bar{p}, \bar{q}$ and $\bar{r}$ are $4, 3$ and $5$ respectively, we can write:

$\bar{s} = 4\bar{p} + 3\bar{q} + 5\bar{r}$

It is also given that the components of $\bar{s}$ along $(-\bar{p}+\bar{q}+\bar{r}), (\bar{p}-\bar{q}+\bar{r})$ and $(-\bar{p}-\bar{q}+\bar{r})$ are $x, y$ and $z$ respectively. Thus:

$\bar{s} = x(-\bar{p}+\bar{q}+\bar{r}) + y(\bar{p}-\bar{q}+\bar{r}) + z(-\bar{p}-\bar{q}+\bar{r})$

Expanding and grouping the terms with $\bar{p}, \bar{q}$ and $\bar{r}$:

$\bar{s} = (-x + y - z)\bar{p} + (x - y - z)\bar{q} + (x + y + z)\bar{r}$

Since $\bar{p}, \bar{q}$ and $\bar{r}$ are non-coplanar vectors, they form a basis. We can equate their corresponding coefficients from both expressions of $\bar{s}$:

$-x + y - z = 4$

$x - y - z = 3$

$x + y + z = 5$

Adding the first and second equations:

$(-x + y - z) + (x - y - z) = 4 + 3$

$-2z = 7 \Rightarrow z = -\dfrac{7}{2}$

Adding the second and third equations:

$(x - y - z) + (x + y + z) = 3 + 5$

$2x = 8 \Rightarrow x = 4$

Substituting the values of $x$ and $z$ into the third equation:

$4 + y - \dfrac{7}{2} = 5$

$y + \dfrac{1}{2} = 5 \Rightarrow y = \dfrac{9}{2}$

We need to find the value of $2x + y + z$:

$2x + y + z = 2(4) + \dfrac{9}{2} + \left(-\dfrac{7}{2}\right)$

$2x + y + z = 8 + \dfrac{2}{2} = 8 + 1 = 9$

Answer: 9

</details>

---

### Q87. MHT CET 2024 (09 May Shift 1)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `1`

If the vectors $a \hat{i}+\hat{j}+\hat{k}, \hat{i}+b \hat{j}+\hat{k}, \hat{i}+\hat{j}+c \hat{k}$ $(a \neq b, c \neq 1)$ are coplanar, then $\dfrac{1}{1-a}+\dfrac{1}{1-b}+\dfrac{1}{1-c}$ has the value $\qquad$

**Options:**

- **(A)** 1
- **(B)** -1
- **(C)** -2
- **(D)** 5

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(A) 1**

**Detailed Solution:**

Since the given vectors are coplanar, their scalar triple product must be zero.

$\begin{vmatrix} a & 1 & 1 \\ 1 & b & 1 \\ 1 & 1 & c \end{vmatrix} = 0$

Applying row operations $R_2 \rightarrow R_2 - R_1$ and $R_3 \rightarrow R_3 - R_1$:

$\begin{vmatrix} a & 1 & 1 \\ 1-a & b-1 & 0 \\ 1-a & 0 & c-1 \end{vmatrix} = 0$

Expanding the determinant along the first row:

$a(b-1)(c-1) - 1(1-a)(c-1) + 1(0 - (1-a)(b-1)) = 0$

$a(1-b)(1-c) + (1-a)(1-c) + (1-a)(1-b) = 0$

Dividing the entire equation by $(1-a)(1-b)(1-c)$:

$\dfrac{a}{1-a} + \dfrac{1}{1-b} + \dfrac{1}{1-c} = 0$

Adding $1$ to both sides:

$\dfrac{a}{1-a} + 1 + \dfrac{1}{1-b} + \dfrac{1}{1-c} = 1$

$\dfrac{1}{1-a} + \dfrac{1}{1-b} + \dfrac{1}{1-c} = 1$

Answer: 1

</details>

---

### Q88. MHT CET 2024 (04 May Shift 1)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `2`

Let $\overline{\mathrm{a}}, \overline{\mathrm{b}}$ and $\overline{\mathrm{c}}$ be three vectors having magnitude 1,1 and 2 respectively. If $\overline{\mathrm{a}} \times(\overline{\mathrm{a}} \times \overline{\mathrm{c}})+\overline{\mathrm{b}}=\overline{0}$, then the acute angle between $\overline{\mathrm{a}}$ and $\overline{\mathrm{c}}$ is

**Options:**

- **(A)** $\dfrac{\pi}{6}$
- **(B)** $\dfrac{\pi}{4}$
- **(C)** $\dfrac{\pi}{3}$
- **(D)** $\dfrac{\pi}{2}$

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(A) $\dfrac{\pi}{6}$**

**Detailed Solution:**

Given $|\overline{a}| = 1$, $|\overline{b}| = 1$, and $|\overline{c}| = 2$.

The given equation is $\overline{a} \times (\overline{a} \times \overline{c}) + \overline{b} = \overline{0}$.

Using the vector triple product expansion, we have:

$(\overline{a} \cdot \overline{c})\overline{a} - (\overline{a} \cdot \overline{a})\overline{c} + \overline{b} = \overline{0}$

Since $|\overline{a}| = 1$, we have $\overline{a} \cdot \overline{a} = 1$. Substituting this gives:

$(\overline{a} \cdot \overline{c})\overline{a} - \overline{c} + \overline{b} = \overline{0}$

Rearranging the terms to isolate $\overline{b}$:

$\overline{b} = \overline{c} - (\overline{a} \cdot \overline{c})\overline{a}$

Taking the square of the magnitude on both sides:

$|\overline{b}|^2 = |\overline{c} - (\overline{a} \cdot \overline{c})\overline{a}|^2$

$|\overline{b}|^2 = |\overline{c}|^2 - 2(\overline{a} \cdot \overline{c})(\overline{c} \cdot \overline{a}) + (\overline{a} \cdot \overline{c})^2 |\overline{a}|^2$

Substituting the given magnitudes $|\overline{a}| = 1$, $|\overline{b}| = 1$, and $|\overline{c}| = 2$:

$1^2 = 2^2 - 2(\overline{a} \cdot \overline{c})^2 + (\overline{a} \cdot \overline{c})^2(1)^2$

$1 = 4 - (\overline{a} \cdot \overline{c})^2$

$(\overline{a} \cdot \overline{c})^2 = 3$

Let $\theta$ be the angle between $\overline{a}$ and $\overline{c}$. Then $\overline{a} \cdot \overline{c} = |\overline{a}||\overline{c}|\cos\theta = (1)(2)\cos\theta = 2\cos\theta$.

Substituting this into the equation:

$(2\cos\theta)^2 = 3$

$4\cos^2\theta = 3$

$\cos^2\theta = \dfrac{3}{4}$

$\cos\theta = \pm\dfrac{\sqrt{3}}{2}$

Since we need the acute angle between $\overline{a}$ and $\overline{c}$, we take the positive value $\cos\theta = \dfrac{\sqrt{3}}{2}$.

Therefore, $\theta = \dfrac{\pi}{6}$.

Answer: $\dfrac{\pi}{6}$

</details>

---

### Q89. MHT CET 2024 (04 May Shift 1)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `1`

The number of distinct real values of $\lambda$, for which the vectors $-\lambda^2 \hat{i}+\hat{j}+\hat{k}, \hat{i}-\lambda^2 \hat{j}+\hat{k}$ and $\hat{i}+\hat{j}-\lambda^2 \hat{k}$ are coplanar, is

**Options:**

- **(A)** zero.
- **(B)** two.
- **(C)** one.
- **(D)** three.

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(B) two.**

**Detailed Solution:**

For the given three vectors to be coplanar, their scalar triple product must be zero. This means the determinant of their components is zero.

$\begin{vmatrix} -\lambda^2 & 1 & 1 \\ 1 & -\lambda^2 & 1 \\ 1 & 1 & -\lambda^2 \end{vmatrix} = 0$

Let $x = -\lambda^2$. The determinant becomes:

$\begin{vmatrix} x & 1 & 1 \\ 1 & x & 1 \\ 1 & 1 & x \end{vmatrix} = 0$

Expanding the determinant along the first row:

$x(x^2 - 1) - 1(x - 1) + 1(1 - x) = 0$

$x^3 - x - x + 1 + 1 - x = 0$

$x^3 - 3x + 2 = 0$

Factoring the polynomial:

$(x - 1)(x^2 + x - 2) = 0$

$(x - 1)^2(x + 2) = 0$

This gives $x = 1$ or $x = -2$.

Substituting $x = -\lambda^2$ back:

Case 1: $-\lambda^2 = 1 \Rightarrow \lambda^2 = -1$
This yields no real values for $\lambda$.

Case 2: $-\lambda^2 = -2 \Rightarrow \lambda^2 = 2$
This yields $\lambda = \pm\sqrt{2}$.

Therefore, there are two distinct real values of $\lambda$ for which the vectors are coplanar.

Answer: two.

</details>

---

### Q90. MHT CET 2024 (04 May Shift 1)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `1`

The value of a for which the volume of parallelepiped formed by $\hat{i}+a \hat{j}+\hat{k}, \hat{j}+a \hat{k}$ and $a \hat{i}+\hat{k}$ becomes local minimum is

**Options:**

- **(A)** $\dfrac{-1}{\sqrt{3}}$
- **(B)** $\dfrac{1}{\sqrt{3}}$
- **(C)** $\sqrt{3}$
- **(D)** $-\sqrt{3}$

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(B) $\dfrac{1}{\sqrt{3}}$**

**Detailed Solution:**

Let the given vectors be $\vec{u} = \hat{i} + a\hat{j} + \hat{k}$, $\vec{v} = \hat{j} + a\hat{k}$, and $\vec{w} = a\hat{i} + \hat{k}$.

The volume of the parallelepiped formed by these vectors is given by $V = |[\vec{u}, \vec{v}, \vec{w}]|$.

$[\vec{u}, \vec{v}, \vec{w}] = \begin{vmatrix} 1 & a & 1 \\ 0 & 1 & a \\ a & 0 & 1 \end{vmatrix}$

$= 1(1 - 0) - a(0 - a^2) + 1(0 - a) = a^3 - a + 1$

Let $f(a) = a^3 - a + 1$.

To find the local extrema, we set $f'(a) = 0$:

$f'(a) = 3a^2 - 1 = 0 \Rightarrow a = \pm \dfrac{1}{\sqrt{3}}$

Now, finding the second derivative:

$f''(a) = 6a$

At $a = \dfrac{1}{\sqrt{3}}$, $f''\left(\dfrac{1}{\sqrt{3}}\right) = \dfrac{6}{\sqrt{3}} > 0$. Thus, $f(a)$ has a local minimum at $a = \dfrac{1}{\sqrt{3}}$.

At $a = -\dfrac{1}{\sqrt{3}}$, $f''\left(-\dfrac{1}{\sqrt{3}}\right) = -\dfrac{6}{\sqrt{3}} < 0$. Thus, $f(a)$ has a local maximum at $a = -\dfrac{1}{\sqrt{3}}$.

Since $f\left(\dfrac{1}{\sqrt{3}}\right) = 1 - \dfrac{2}{3\sqrt{3}} > 0$, the volume $V = |f(a)| = f(a)$ in the neighborhood of $a = \dfrac{1}{\sqrt{3}}$.

Therefore, the volume of the parallelepiped is a local minimum at $a = \dfrac{1}{\sqrt{3}}$.

Answer: $\dfrac{1}{\sqrt{3}}$

</details>

---

### Q91. MHT CET 2024 (04 May Shift 1)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `2`

Let p and q be the position vectors of P and Q respectively, with respect to O and $|\overline{\mathrm{p}}|=\mathrm{p},|\overline{\mathrm{q}}|=\mathrm{q}$. The points R and S divide PQ internally and externally in the ratio $2: 3$ respectively. If $O R$ and OS are perpendiculars, then

**Options:**

- **(A)** $9 \mathrm{p}^2=4 \mathrm{q}^2$
- **(B)** $4 \mathrm{p}^2=9 \mathrm{q}^2$
- **(C)** $9 \mathrm{p}=4 \mathrm{q}$
- **(D)** $4 \mathrm{p}=9 \mathrm{q}$

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(A) $9 \mathrm{p}^2=4 \mathrm{q}^2$**

**Detailed Solution:**

The position vector of point $R$ dividing $PQ$ internally in the ratio $2:3$ is given by:

$\vec{r} = \dfrac{2\vec{q} + 3\vec{p}}{2 + 3} = \dfrac{3\vec{p} + 2\vec{q}}{5}$

The position vector of point $S$ dividing $PQ$ externally in the ratio $2:3$ is given by:

$\vec{s} = \dfrac{2\vec{q} - 3\vec{p}}{2 - 3} = \dfrac{2\vec{q} - 3\vec{p}}{-1} = 3\vec{p} - 2\vec{q}$

Since $OR$ and $OS$ are perpendicular, their dot product must be zero:

$\vec{r} \cdot \vec{s} = 0$

$\left( \dfrac{3\vec{p} + 2\vec{q}}{5} \right) \cdot (3\vec{p} - 2\vec{q}) = 0$

$(3\vec{p} + 2\vec{q}) \cdot (3\vec{p} - 2\vec{q}) = 0$

$9|\vec{p}|^2 - 4|\vec{q}|^2 = 0$

Given $|\vec{p}| = p$ and $|\vec{q}| = q$, we get:

$9p^2 - 4q^2 = 0$

$9p^2 = 4q^2$

Answer: $9 \mathrm{p}^2=4 \mathrm{q}^2$

</details>

---

### Q92. MHT CET 2024 (04 May Shift 1)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `1`

Let $\bar{a}=\alpha \hat{i}+3 \hat{j}-\hat{k}, \bar{b}=3 \hat{i}-\beta \hat{j}+4 \hat{k} \quad$ and $\overline{\mathrm{c}}=\hat{\mathrm{i}}+2 \hat{\mathrm{j}}-2 \hat{\mathrm{k}}$, where $\alpha, \beta \in \mathbb{R}$, be three vectors.
If the projection of $\overline{\mathrm{a}}$ on $\overline{\mathrm{c}}$ is $\dfrac{10}{3}$ and $\bar{b} \times \bar{c}=-6 \hat{i}+10 \hat{j}+7 \hat{k}$, then the value of $2 \alpha+\beta$ is

**Options:**

- **(A)** 3
- **(B)** 4
- **(C)** 5
- **(D)** 6

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(C) 5**

**Detailed Solution:**

The projection of $\vec{a}$ on $\vec{c}$ is given by $\dfrac{\vec{a} \cdot \vec{c}}{|\vec{c}|}$.
